- **Grafana alert summaries are the strongest case in the rule, not an exception to it.** The reasoning that excuses log lines — the reader has the repo open — is exactly inverted for an alert read on a phone in Slack. Ten summaries carried trailing tokens appended to otherwise actionable text; the tokens went, the instructions stayed. Pushed live and read back: 48 rules, zero carrying vocabulary.
- **Surfaces the rule named but nothing checked** are now checked: Prometheus metric HELP text, Grafana panel descriptions, compose `${VAR:?message}` errors, and `infra/` shell as well as Python — a textfile exporter's `# HELP` lines are printed by `printf`, and widening the scanned *directory* without widening the *file types* is how the trophy example (`spec 00054/T0058`, the very string used to demonstrate the path-carve-out fix) survived two sweeps.
- **Several leaks were added the same evening**, by the T0021 and T0027 iterations, while this topic sat in the queue. That is the argument for enforcement over a one-time sweep, in one line.
- **Two boundaries decided rather than left implicit**: log lines out of scope (verified, not assumed — no `logger.*` literal carries vocabulary today); a token inside a file path is an operand, not a reference, so it stays.
- **Caught in my own work twice.** A slice-then-replace edit deleted the CLI test function and the follow-up `.replace()` silently no-op'd, because I omitted the `assert old in s` that `agent-ops.md` requires — the suite dropping from 51 failures to 21 with no corresponding fix is what exposed it. Later, a regex meant to tidy `" ."` glued a sentence to `.claude/rules/...`; a targeted re-grep for that shape found the one instance.

## 2026-07-27 — Alloy v1.17.1 → v1.18.0 across the fleet, and what the run taught the skill ([[T0081]])

- **All four Alloy containers bumped in canary order** (ops → NAS → capture secondary → capture primary), `4f6ddc56ffdc` → `491b0578c049`, every one landing with `RestartCount` 0. The `/zcrypto-bump-alloy` skill's first real run, which is what this topic was waiting on.
- **No protected service was touched, and that was verified rather than assumed.** Passing each host's *currently-running* capture digest kept the capture-compose render idempotent — it never entered the changed set, so the `restart capture service` handler could not fire. On the primary, `--skip-tags engine` kept the engine play out entirely: the live trade engine's `StartedAt` is byte-identical across the whole run, and both capture daemons kept writing parquet throughout (17 and 22 files in the 3 minutes after their bumps).
- **Config risk was measured, not assumed.** v1.18.0 ships two breaking changes, both `otelcol.*`; the fleet uses only `loki.*` and `prometheus.*`. All three `config.alloy` files were then dry-started against the new binary with `alloy validate` before any host was touched — worth doing regardless, since the capture config had been changed hours earlier that same evening.
- **The run corrected four things in the written procedure**, which is the entire argument for validating a deploy skill by running it:
  1. **`docker compose up -d` needs `sudo`** — `alloy-secrets.env` is `0600 zcrypto-alloy`. The unprivileged form fails *before* touching the container, so the old one keeps running and no dark window opens; but the skill's command omitted it.
  2. **Every ops recreate trips `Ops · ERROR logs`** — the *outgoing* container logs two `service=remotecfg … err="noop client"` errors on shutdown, and that rule is host-wide with `for: 0s` over 15 m. Telling it from a real fault needs a specific check: the lines are stamped ~200 ms *before* the new container's `StartedAt`, and the new container's logs are clean.
  3. **Alloy goes quiet after startup**, so the prescribed `{host=…, container="alloy"}` freshness check reads empty on a healthy host bumped 30 minutes earlier. The liveness proof has to accept any container's line — caught by refusing to treat an empty filtered query as an absent event, and finding the positive trace (`liquidations` lines still flowing, `loki_sent_entries` climbing).
  4. **A fresh container reports `samples_total=0`** until its first 60 s scrape lands; reading too eagerly looks like a dead shipper.
- **Verified per host before moving on**: new digest with zero restarts; `failed_total` 0 and `samples_total` climbing on `127.0.0.1:12345`; `up` present in Cloud; six `process_*` families. Host-specific — the ops liquidations poller still scraped, and the NAS's next `archive-pull` cycle logging `checked=9215 ok=9215 failed=0` after the apply bounced it. End state: **48 alert rules, none firing.**
- **The bump also carried the previous iteration's cosmetic tail.** Every converge landed the T0096 `Description=` rewordings for that host's timers — exactly the "lands at each role's next natural converge" prediction, arriving within the hour rather than someday.

## 2026-07-27 — two drills, and a real incident that arrived on its own ([[T0035]], [[T0008]], [[T0049]], [[T0101]])

- **[[T0035]] resolved by drill, after two half-verifications that never tested the handler.** The code fix was landed and regression-tested; what had never happened was the handler for a *rejected* connect attempt executing in production — zero `attempt [2-9]` lines across the full Loki retention, because every observed reconnect succeeded first try. A throwaway capture on the ops node (same pinned digest, real Kraken, isolated dir, no Loki creds, no dead-man URL) closed that.
- **Kraken supplied the fault unprompted.** The sandbox's *very first* connect was rejected with `server rejected WebSocket connection: HTTP 503` — this topic's originating fault, arriving within seconds without being induced. That says the 503 is more common than production logs suggest; production has simply always succeeded on the retry. A `docker network disconnect` then supplied the `OSError` arm, so both halves of `except (WebSocketException, OSError, TimeoutError)` were exercised.
- **What the sustained blackout proved**, none of it previously observed: backoff `1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 60, 60, 60, 60` (exponential, capped); the `_RECONNECT_ERROR_EVERY` line at attempt 10; **`restarts=0` across ~6 minutes and 15 attempts** — the actual claim; clean resubscribe-and-resume on reconnect; and **data consistency across the fault**, with the spanning hour's book *and* trades `.parquet` both hash-verifying against their manifests.
- **[[T0008]]'s alert leg validated, and deliberately scored as not closing the topic.** Injecting `zcrypto_capture_book_desynced{pair="DRILL"} 1` through the textfile transport built the day before drove the full path: Cloud → `Pending` → `Alerting` → Slack naming the pair → resolved on removal, with all 24 real pair instances staying `Normal` throughout. But no desync occurred, so `resubscribe_book` never ran — the safety net is proven, the defect behind it is not. Recorded as a partial rather than counted as a validation that did not happen.
- **A drill's latency is not the real latency.** The injected series alerted in 6.4 min against the summary's stated ~20; `min_over_time` aggregates only present samples, so a brand-new series satisfies it immediately while a real desync flips an *existing* series and carries a full window of zeros. The summary is right for the real case. Recorded in T0049, because the same trap applies to any alert-path injection.
- **A real incident arrived mid-session and is the better teacher.** *Reconciler · primary gap rate high* fired at 09:48Z — **41 minutes before the drills**, so unrelated. The primary needed **2,318 s (~39 min)** of healing in 6 h while its own `zcrypto_capture_gap_seconds_total` read **zero**. Windows of 1 h / 6 h / 24 h / 7 d measured 0 / 2318 / 2313 / 2312 s: one event, already over, not a trend. `healed_gap_seconds_total` matched it, so the secondary minted every second — **no data loss**, the redundant-capture design working silently.
- **The disagreement is the finding, and it under-reports.** The daemon booked no gap for a period the reconciler measured at 39 minutes; the arithmetic (~39 s per pair per reconnect across 5 reconnects and 12 pairs) points at reconnect silence never being booked, though that is unverified against the ledger. Registered as [[T0101]] rather than absorbed, because everything keyed on the daemon's gap counter — including the operator's sense of how healthy the primary is — reads clean through a 39-minute hole.
- **[[T0049]] gains the half it was missing.** Its matrix rehearsed *inducing* faults; it had no entry for reconstructing one already over and silently absorbed. Added as a scenario with the sequence this incident actually required — bound it across widening windows before calling it a trend, establish loss-or-no-loss *first*, cross-check the two producers against each other, and rule out your own same-day changes by timestamp (without which this would have been misattributed to the Alloy bump, the cheapest wrong answer available). Both drill methods are recorded there for reuse.

## 2026-07-27 — desync recovery survives its first attempt failing ([[T0008]], spec 00072)

- **Recovery is now a bounded ladder**: the existing transition resubscribe, then 3 retries at 5/10/20 s, then exactly one escalation to a full reconnect, then stop. Built on the owner's ruling to do (a)+(c) as one design rather than two — each rung clears strictly more state than the last, which is the whole reason escalation is meaningful.
- **Retries are driven by desync STATE, never by protocol responses**, and that is the load-bearing choice. A snapshot that fails its own checksum produces no error frame at all — Kraken is satisfied, the protocol succeeded, the book is still wrong. Only "still desynced N seconds later" can see it, which is also why `req_id` correlation is not a substitute (registered as [[T0102]] for its separate observability value).
- **The parameters are unfitted, and the spec says so rather than implying otherwise.** `zcrypto_capture_resubscribes_total` and `_gap_seconds_total` both read **0 on both hosts** — the 2026-07-13 root-cause fix took desyncs to zero, so there is no heal-latency distribution to fit. They come from the two constraints that are known: a grace generous against any snapshot round-trip, and a total ladder short enough that the dead-man it withholds **for all 12 pairs** stays inside its grace.
- **The dead-man is the real budget.** While any pair is desynced, `is_healthy()` is False and the ping is gated on it, so the entire ladder runs with that host's hc.io watchdog dark for every pair. That is why the ladder is measured in seconds — a "patient" ladder silently trades one stuck pair for a fleet-wide dead-man outage.
- **Drilled against real Kraken, both halves.** Hold 45 s: rung 1 fired **once** (guard held, no storm), retries at +23.4 / +5.002 / +10.004 s, pair healed before escalation. Hold 600 s: escalation at **+20.002 s**, `force_reconnect` reached `stream()`'s existing `ConnectionClosed` path, and over the following ~4 minutes with the pair still held desynced there was **no second escalation**. `retries=3, escalations=1, restarts=0`; desync→escalation **58.3 s** against the predicted ~55 s.
- **The terminal state was the part worth drilling.** A cycling ladder turns one stuck pair into a reconnect loop, and every reconnect drops all 12 pairs — strictly worse than the defect. No unit test establishes that in a live process.
- **Two drill runs failed first, both in the instrument rather than the ladder.** Faking only the handler's return value left the book reporting healthy, so `was_desynced` read False on every forced failure and the transition guard re-fired rung 1 twice in 0.5 s — the exact storm it exists to prevent — while the state-reading recovery loop never engaged. Setting `book.desynced` as well, but on a book whose data was genuinely valid, let the next update recompute a good CRC and heal it in milliseconds, before the 20 s grace could elapse; the daemon stayed healthy and kept writing parquet, which is precisely what made it look like a pass. The knob's unit became a **duration**, since a pair is stuck for a length of time and neither a count nor a flag expresses that.
- **The generalisable lesson, recorded in [[T0049]]:** a fault injector that does not move the state the system actually keys on does not simulate the fault — it simulates a different one, and does so convincingly enough to read as a pass.
- **Whole-branch review then found two high-severity defects, both in the driver loop rather than the ladder's arithmetic, and neither reachable by either drill.** *The escalation bound leaked, and the escalation itself was the leak*: `note_recovered` cleared the escalation record, so "one per pair per hour" bound only a pair that stayed *continuously* desynced — while the likeliest healer of an escalated pair is that escalation's own reconnect, which forces a fresh snapshot for everything. Escalate → reconnect → heal → record erased → escalate again ~55 s later. Simulated against the real ladder: a pair desyncing every 10 min escalated **6×/hour against the intended 1**, a flapping pair **51×/hour** — **72** and **~610** reconnects/hour fleet-wide across 12 pairs, against 12. Those are state-machine ceilings, not a steady state: the ladder has no clock, so the reconnect's own downtime goes uncharged. The 6:1 over-run is the finding, and at ~39 s of silence per pair per reconnect ([[T0101]]'s arithmetic from one incident, not a fitted number) it is the "strictly worse than the defect" outcome D5 warns about. Neither drill could have caught it: drill A never escalated, so there was no record to erase, and drill B held the pair desynced throughout — the one shape where the old code was correct.
- **The second: one pair's exception starved eleven.** The `try` wrapped the whole sweep and `books` is insertion-ordered, so a raising pair deterministically starved every pair after it — this topic's own defect reintroduced for 11 pairs by 1. Compounding it, `note_attempt` was skipped when the call raised, so the pair retried every tick forever *without* ever reaching rung 3 (57 retries in 5 min, 0 escalations). The trigger is self-inflicted: pair A's `force_reconnect` closes the socket pair B then sends into.
- **Mutation testing was the sharpest finding, and it took three passes to actually land.** Deleting `note_desync`, `note_recovered`, or the `recovery` argument entirely — a ladder nobody arms — passed the whole suite. The new suite's own docstring claimed to cover that seam while no test called `_handle_book_message`: the [[T0035]] / [[T0100]] trap one level down, inside the branch built to escape it.
- **Calling the real handler did not cover the real caller.** A second review re-ran the mutations and found `recovery` could still be dropped from `_consume`'s call with everything green — the tests passed the ladder in explicitly, so none of them ever executed the production call site, and both functions defaulted the parameter to `None` so the drop was silent rather than a `TypeError`. Fixed on both axes: the argument is now required, and a test drives `_consume` itself so that `TypeError` is reached by the suite. Five mutations, each verified dead by re-running it.
- **The mutation harness itself was the third defect, and it faked two passes.** `git archive HEAD` sandboxes are useless here: the editable install's `.pth` puts the repo on `sys.path`, so `pytest` inside a sandbox imports the **repo's** `cli` and `tests` — every "survived" and "killed" verdict measured unmutated code. Caught only because a mutation that provably raises `TypeError` when invoked directly still reported green under pytest. Mutations now run in-repo against a committed tree, restored with `git checkout --` after each.
- **Incomplete wiring broke six existing tests** — the scheduler referenced a `recovery` that was never constructed. Caught by running the capture suites rather than only the new ones, which is the whole argument for not scoping a test run to the files you touched.
- **Merged unrolled, deliberately.** Capture-daemon code on the unbackfillable path reaches the fleet only through an image build, the ≥24 h secondary canary bake, then the primary re-pin; the deploy is its own attended step.

## 2026-07-27 — a stream that is connected, subscribed, and silent ([[T0101]], spec 00073)

- **The 2026-07-27 blackout was a Kraken WebSocket service restart, and the daemon was structurally unable to see it.** Both hosts lost all 12 book streams 07:01:04 → ~07:04:35 UTC — every pair's last message within **0.454658 s** of every other — then received an application-layer `1012 (service restart)` close **19.65 ms apart** (`07:04:49.352183069Z` primary, `07:04:49.332529441Z` secondary), and the primary's next connect was answered `HTTP 503`. Exactly two such frames exist in 19.3 days of retention (07-13 and 07-27, both Mondays, 14 days apart) with **07-20 clean as a negative control** — a signature worth acting on, not a schedule.
- **Every liveness signal answered correctly while the data was absent.** The socket stayed open (a 1012 cannot arrive on a dead one), the `websockets` keepalive completed **≥11 ping/pong round trips** at its 20 s/20 s defaults, no gap window existed — so the healthchecks.io dead-man **pinged green through a total 12-pair blackout on both hosts**. `gap_seconds_total` read `0.0` on all 12 pairs across 97.57 h of uptime containing 33 reconnects, and neither container log held a single `gap start` line in its whole 4-day life.
- **T0101's own diagnosis was wrong, and correcting it was the useful part.** It read the event as unbooked *reconnect* silence at "~39 s per pair per reconnect". Measured reconnect cost is **single-digit seconds** — 0.002–2.82 s close-frame-to-first-book-event, 2.276–6.204 s close-to-first-processed-message; the two methods disagree on the interval but not on the order of magnitude — 2,318/12 ≈ 193 s is **one** silence window per pair. The defect was never reconnect silence but **connected-but-silent upstream** silence, which no reconnect accounting would have found. The falsified 39 s figure is withdrawn from spec `00072` D5 and from `desync_recovery.py`, where the escalation-bound argument survives *a fortiori* at the smaller number.
- **Its "Not data loss — and that is verified" was also false, and the verification was circular** — it checked the healed counter against the healable counter, both summed from the same ledger. Measured against the parquet: **2,437.147792 s** of L2 permanently absent across 12 pairs, against **82.955463 s** actually healed of the 2,311.536587 s booked. A CRITICAL page had already fired at **09:18:35Z**, thirty minutes *before* the warning the topic was written from.
- **Three changes shipped.** A per-pair book staleness watchdog stamped at `last_seen` rather than at detection (stamping at `now` would discard the threshold from every outage — the same under-reporting direction as the defect); the venue's `status` channel recorded instead of dropped unlogged; and a `1012` flooring the first reconnect delay at 5 s per Kraken's guidance, after the primary's 1.0 s attempt earned that `HTTP 503`.
- **The threshold is derived, not guessed.** Worst *natural* intra-hour book spacing fleet-wide is **12.196 s** (ETH/BTC, the thinnest leg; largest natural hourly max **12.299 s** over 104 segments), so 30 s is ~2.4× the binding pair's p99 — and it equals the reconciler's `--min-gap-seconds`, so the two producers finally measure the same thing rather than merely adjacent things.
- **Silence gets its OWN window in `GapMonitor`**, for the same reason the disk watermark has one: `start_gap` is idempotent per pair, so routing silence through it would let a concurrent `checksum_resync` gap swallow it, and whichever closed first would book the other's duration. The two faults genuinely co-occur.
- **The dead-man is deliberately NOT gated on silence yet**, and the consequence is stated rather than glossed: a repeat of 07-27 is now correctly counted and still pages nobody. `is_healthy()` gates the ping for all 12 pairs on both hosts, so a threshold fitted to ~4 days of thin-leg data would trade a metric gap for a liveness outage. A test asserts the current behaviour so reversing it cannot happen by accident. Registered as [[T0105]].
- **Mutation caught what review did not: a watchdog nobody starts passed all 22 tests.** Every property test drives the loop body directly, so all of them hold on a loop `_run` never schedules — [[T0100]]'s defect in another costume, and the second time this class has survived a branch built to escape it. The guard parses `_run`'s AST rather than grepping the module, because a grep for `_staleness_loop(` matches the definition and would pass on the exact mutation it exists to catch.
- **Two repo comments were rewritten because they argued against the fix.** `tests/test_infra_alert_rules.py` excluded `gap_seconds_total` on the grounds that it "measured zero across all 24 series" and that an open gap is "covered twice over — the dead-man and the desync rule". The zero **was** the blind spot, and neither covering mechanism saw it.
- **The new gauge was added to the Alloy keep-regex in the same change.** It is an allow-list with no wildcard, so a published-but-unadmitted series is dropped at remote-write and looks identical to a producer that never ran — the [[T0051]] trap, avoided here only because the exclusion-list staleness test failed loudly first.
- **Three topics split out rather than left inside an archived file**: [[T0103]] (the reconciler books unfilled silence as healed, and double-counts 2,187.03 s into both a "covered" and a "nobody covered" counter), [[T0104]] (the panel emits a frozen book across the hole — 212 rows with **2 distinct `mid`** against 8 across the hour's other zero-update seconds, already materialized into the research feature set), and [[T0105]].
- **One thing deliberately left unexcluded**: both hosts resolve `ws.kraken.com` to the *identical* Cloudflare anycast set, so host independence is established but **network-path independence is not** — and `HTTP 503` is what a CDN edge returns when it cannot reach origin. It changes no decision here, since the watchdog fires either way, but it bears on whether a mirror on a different provider would have covered the loss.

## 2026-07-27 — the corpus learns to refine itself (spec 00074, T0099, T0084 fallout)

- **`/zcrypto-refine-rules` exists and its first run is this entry's iteration**: harvest → joint graduation → staleness sweep → condense → lossless verify, user-invoked only, with the watermark a git trailer prose cannot fake. The cold spec+plan review it introduced ran three times today and returned FIX FIRST or LOSSY each time — every verdict correct.
- **Graduation: 12 memory items walked jointly** — 7 graduated (4 one-liners into `agent-ops`, the model-attribution mechanism into `commit-messages`, the memo-authority + registered-but-unqueued lesson into `open-topics`, the write-time deferral sweep as a mandatory `open-pr` step), 4 dropped as duplicates verified at equal-or-greater strength, 1 stays. **The case study passed**: both seeded fixtures traversed the full pipeline and took joint dispositions — one as the general drift-is-not-license form, one as mechanization (P6) rather than more prose.
- **The staleness sweep (10 read-only checkers, 15 findings) was all real**: a census-shaped CLAUDE.md bullet three commands stale, `docs/universe/` missing from the docs map, dead and circular cross-references in three skills, a skill whose own mandated loop its allowlist forbade, a release skill asserting opposite facts about develop's protection in two steps (settled by measurement: PR-protected, step 17 wins), and a changelog template whose `v` its own extraction awk required.
- **The D-number enforcement gap closed and immediately paid**: the rule always claimed `spec` D-numbers as guarded vocabulary; the test had no pattern for them. Adding it flagged four live leaks (soak header, soak note, backfill error message, one alert summary) — each reworded to keep the semantics with the token in an adjacent comment, and the reworded summary pushed to Grafana Cloud and read back the same evening.
- **Dependabot de-grouped on the owner's ruling** — per-ecosystem PRs on the pre-group schedules recovered from git history — resolving [[T0099]] by construction: the is-the-group-honored question is moot for a config that declares no group.
- **`fleet-deploys.md` went 6,261 → 4,664 bytes in one day**: rollout mechanics to their single home in the `zcrypto-rollout-image` skill, the Reboots section (host posture, not deploy law) to `fleet.md` — with the owner catching what the sweep missed: "the reverse of the image-rollout order" was false; reboots use the **same** canary order.
- **The P7 gate returned LOSSY twice and was right twice.** The T0084 shrink had deleted a bullet that also served engine converges; the round's own diff carried three losses — a routing sentence pointing reboots at the file they left, a graduated clause that **inverted its own lesson's ground truth** (naming the artifact class the lesson had measured stale), and a lesson half that had landed nowhere. Every changed line, not only removals — that scope is why the second and third were catchable at all.
- **The skill corrected itself mid-run** (the T0081 pattern): sequential decision gates — fact-collection may run ahead, but the next step's findings are held until the previous step's dispositions close; presenting two open sets collided them on the user's desk.
- **Net always-loaded: 33,693 → 34,963 bytes (+1,270, presented for the owner's OK at close — growth is never assumed approved)** — seven graduated lessons plus the P7 repairs cost more than the Reboots relocation recovered, and the round reports the measured sign rather than the intended one. The memory inbox closed at one item.

## 2026-07-28 — the instruments that measured the blackout, corrected (T0104, T0102, T0103, T0087, T0043)

- **Two of the 2026-07-27 blackout's three remediation children shipped** — T0103 and T0104; T0105 stays deliberately deferred — alongside [[T0102]], which is a T0008 child rather than a blackout one. The theme across all of them is the one T0101 itself ended on: every instrument that looked at that event reported it wrongly, and each in the reassuring direction.
- **The panel's dense grid now says when a book is frozen** (`stale_seconds`, spec `00052` generation 2): the 1-second grid emits a row per second whether or not data exists, so an archive hole produces carried-forward rows of a stale book that `updates == 0` alone cannot distinguish from a quiet second — and the hour still emits its full 3,600 either way. Measured on the real 07:00 hour: **174** rows above the 30 s threshold, 3,425 below, and **1 null** — the anchor sidecar predates the column, and polars drops nulls from *both* sides of a `> 30` filter, so the null is the hazard worth publishing. The column addition is a **generation** change: without the `SCHEMA_VERSION` bump a mixed 19/20-column tree raises `SchemaError` on any multi-hour scan, even one touching only old columns, and `_check_generation` would have waved it through because none of its four compared keys changed. The re-materialization is an attended sequencing requirement — deploy and regenerate in one window, or the hourly timer aborts and its dead-man pages meanwhile.
- **The capture daemon correlates its resubscribes by `req_id`**: all four reply categories now reach `note_reply` — the two acks it discarded outright, and the two errors it only printed — so a rejected resubscribe is correlated and counted rather than merely logged. That is T0102's observability half and its prevention half (the `subscribe` waits on the `unsubscribe_ack`), since a resubscribe that silently failed is indistinguishable from one that silently succeeded until the next gap.
- **The reconciler's counters measure the splice rather than the window that admitted it.** `secondary_covers` admits a gap on one secondary `update` row anywhere inside it; one row admits a window, it does not fill one. The blackout hour claimed **2,311.536587 s** healed against a measured **82.955463 s** — 3.59% — and LINK/EUR booked 209.493793 s on 6 update rows inside a spliced block spanning 1.638743 s — the updates themselves span 5 ms. `measure_residual` now re-runs the window arithmetic over the frame actually minted, `healed_seconds` becomes claimed − unfilled, and the unfilled remainder reaches the provenance sidecar, which had been writing a literal `residual_gaps: []` on every minted hour in the archive.
- **The fix had to avoid replacing a heal over-count with a loss over-count**, since `both_streams_silent` already books the fleet-dark intersection as window × stream count and 2,187.027326 of that event's stream-seconds sat in a "we covered it" and a "nobody covered it" counter simultaneously. Each side now subtracts what the **ledger records** the other already booked — never a fresh recomputation, which review measured wrong in both directions: a pair whose mirrors land after the fleet decision had its whole loss deleted from a total nobody added it to, and an unreadable segment suppresses the detector for a cycle, so a pair minting meanwhile booked first and the fleet record booked the same seconds again. Attribution is order-independent, and both orders are tests. `residual_seconds` also joined the per-(pair, kind, hour) dedup, because a `would_mint` → `minted` flip carries the same measured residual on both records and the second step reads to the CRITICAL page as a fresh permanent-loss event.
- **The correction was validated on a known answer before its own verdicts were read.** The 2026-07-17 drill hour — primary deliberately stopped, secondary genuinely up — measures 15,509.529138 s healed against 15,509.529138 s claimed, **0.0000% fiction**: the stricter measure does not slander a genuine heal. The blackout hour's 82.955463 s then agrees with T0103's figure **to the microsecond** — re-derived by a second route over the minted parquet rather than independently corroborated: both are the same window arithmetic at the same threshold, so the agreement confirms the implementation, not the premise. The topic's own lifetime-fiction figure was wrong (2,252.718188 s reconciled with neither measurement beside it); measured, it is **2,228.581124 s, 12.5053%** of the ledger's 17,821.065725 s, and the file was rewritten in place.
- **A suspected symmetric defect turned out not to exist, and was dropped with the measurement as the reason**: all 18 `trade_deficit` records enumerated, every one a *secondary* deficit (`trades_added = 0` in all 18, 1,926 rows). The canonical trade stream lost nothing, so their `residual_seconds: 0.0` is correct. Permanent trade loss cannot appear in such a record at all — rows absent from both mirrors are invisible to a cross-host union, and `backfill-trades` already measures them as `unrecoverable`.
- **The trade backfill gained the two buckets a real outcome could fall between.** `mint_failed` counts rows the REST served for an hour whose mint then raised — previously in no printed bucket at all — and it is reported but **never subtracted** from the post-mint accounting check, because a fetched-but-unminted row is retryable and an explained absence would silence the sweep's strongest check on exactly the failure it exists for. `hours_repaired_after_loss` counts settled hours minted with no prior trades final while a book final **spanning** the same hour existed: the signature of a genuinely lost segment that the REST backfill would otherwise repair silently.
- **That signature's first cut over-claimed, and our own operations produce the false positive.** A book final's existence proves only that one event landed *somewhere* in the hour — a capture restart at :45 leaves one too, and every image converge restarts capture. The witness now reads the final's first and last `ts` and requires it to span the hour within 60 s at each edge; an unreadable or empty final witnesses nothing, since the question is whether the stream can be *proven* connected.
- **Attended tails, left registered rather than merged behind**: the panel re-materialization (T0104), the reconciler's two alert rules plus a third this work made stale (the CRITICAL page still sends the operator to look for a `both_streams_silent` record it no longer needs), the ledger's existing fiction (all three T0103), and the export of `hours_repaired_after_loss` (T0043). The capture image roll carrying T0102 is tracked in the memo's live rollout block rather than in a topic — the same shape T0008's deploy took, and the reason the memo is the in-flight authority. The ledger correction's shape is now decided — an appended note record carrying **no** counter field, because any record that lowers a monotone counter is a decrease, and Prometheus reads a decrease as a reset and reports the post-reset value as fresh healing: louder than the fiction it corrects.
- **Two process findings, both agreed with the owner rather than argued away.** First: three of these components met `spec-plan-locations.md`'s **substantive** bar — a schema-generation bump with a clock threaded across hour boundaries, a counter-semantics rewrite across four modules carrying an exactly-once attribution invariant, and an async ack-correlation state machine on the unbackfillable capture path — and all three ran the *trivial* path, so none got a committed spec, a committed plan, or the cold spec+plan review at the Fable floor that path prescribes for capture and canonical data. The evidence is this branch's own history: three defects fixed before merge were introduced by earlier commits on it. Not backfilled, deliberately — a spec written afterwards documents what was built instead of shaping it. Second: the `Reviewed-by:` trailer is missing on nine non-merge commits, three of them code. The reviews did happen; the durable evidence did not land, and the closeout-commit fallback cannot reach three of them because they land after it. Recorded in the PR body as a one-off on the owner's ruling rather than fixed by rewriting nine commits mid-history.
- **A four-dimension review before merge found five code defects the per-commit rounds had not** — archive correctness, panel/capture correctness, documentation truthfulness, and adversarial deploy readiness, run in parallel against the whole diff. The sharpest was the per-stream booking introduced *by this iteration*: it computed each stream's window from whichever mirrors were readable that cycle, so a stream whose second mirror arrived a cycle late had its entire single-mirror silence booked as permanent loss — 2,860 s over-booked against the old code's 465 s, unbounded where the old error was bounded by the intersection width, and reachable by the repo's own mandated primary-first pair-add order. The lesson is not that reviews find things; it is that **a review scoped to one commit cannot see a defect whose inputs come from the deploy environment**, and only the adversarial deploy dimension asked what the fleet would actually hand the code.

## 2026-07-29 — the config that never reached the process, and the triggers nobody was watching (T0107, T0109, T0110, T0106, T0108, T0103, T0105)

- **The deferred capture payload rolled** as `sha256:99faf165…`, secondary 00:52:53Z and primary 07:36:13Z after a 6 h 43 m bake with a strong-form prune (`deleted=210`). Both payload items were verified from the image's own surface rather than its tag. `continuity.py` on the pulled copy: 0 missing, 0 truncated, worst stream 0.0123% against the 0.1% bar — the mid-hour restart produced no truncated hour, because the stop→start window was 29 s.
- **Spec `00073`'s measurement half had been emitted and dropped since the day it was built.** Both capture hosts published `seconds_since_last_book_message` and `venue_status_total` to a keep-regex that never admitted them, so the fleet's answer to the 2026-07-27 blackout could not be alerted on or graphed. All five previously-undelivered series now arrive.
- **A `config.alloy` edit reached the host and not the process, on all three tiers.** A single-file bind mount binds the inode; Ansible's `copy` writes atomically and replaces it, so a running container reads a file no longer in the host tree — indefinitely, while every converge reports `changed`. Measured on `zcrypto-ops` right after a clean converge: host and container `sha256` **and** inode both differing. Fixed structurally — all three tiers mount the config *directory*, with a change-triggered `reload alloy` handler and a CI guard against regressing to a single-file mount.
- **That trap was already documented, and that is the actual finding.** Spec `00071` records the same mechanism in nearly the same words and chose attended-recreate, explicitly warning against a handler. The ruling never reached the operator-facing rules, so it was re-derived in production during the converge that was fixing the layer above it. **A decision recorded only in a spec is a decision nobody executing will read.**
- **Every ordinary check reported success while the container read a stale inode** — Ansible said `changed`, the host `grep` found the term, Alloy's reload genuinely succeeded, the exporter emitted every series. Each was accurate and each measured the wrong side of the mount. Only comparing host-vs-container `sha256` exposed it, so that comparison is now the verification.
- **The detection gap became its own converge-time assert (T0110)**, deliberately *outside* `when: <tier>_alloy_digest is defined` — inside, it would be skipped by exactly the converge that causes the drift. Its first implementation compared against `lookup('ansible.builtin.file', …) | hash('sha256')`; the file lookup **strips the trailing newline**, so it failed on a host byte-identical to the repo and would have failed on every converge forever. A permanently-red assert gets deleted, not investigated — the both-directions verification is what caught it.
- **A liveness gauge that means what it says** (T0106): the ship-success timestamp stamps only on a successful *non-empty* push, so it reads stale whenever logging is merely quiet, which is what healthy looks like. Measured on a green host during the bake: `last_success` 39 minutes stale while the new `last_cycle_timestamp_seconds` read 0 s. The rollout's abort row now reads the honest one and the old gauge is marked explicitly *not* an abort signal.
- **The unwitnessed-loss exposure was measured rather than argued** (T0108): a 400 h detect-only sweep over the full mirror overlap, using post-fix records so the split was a pure ledger read — one event in ~15 days, **6.821701 s** reaching no counter. Decided (a), leave it ledger-only; the residual counter's HELP now says it is a floor on permanent loss rather than the whole of it. That also settled a figure this topic had reversed itself on twice: 9.746002 s and 6.821701 s were each correct for their own code generation.
- **The pre-fix over-statement is recorded, not erased** (T0103). Appending a record that *lowered* `healed_gap_seconds_total` would read to Prometheus as a counter reset and make `increase()` report the post-reset value as fresh healing — louder than the fiction it corrects. Instead a `state: "correction"` note carrying no counter field, proven counter-neutral against the live ledger before the write and confirmed unchanged by the next cycle's re-derivation.
- **Triggers that nobody was watching became triggers that fire.** Two topics held items gated on events months away with no mechanism to notice them. The venue-status half is now a Grafana alert on `system != "online"` — a *negative* match, because the payload shape for a real outage is unobserved and an enumeration would silently miss whatever Kraken actually sends. Its first form used `increase()`, which cannot fire on a series that is born at 1 and never steps: it would have sat green through the very event it exists for.
- **`infra/runbooks/README.md` establishes the day-2 pattern** the drill matrix (T0049) will fill: one `##` section per trigger, anchored on what the operator already holds — the alert `uid` from Slack or the slug from a code comment — four fixed parts, two section kinds (ALERT, KNOWN LIMITATION), an explicitly *checkable* retirement condition per section, and a stated boundary that it is not a backlog. T0049's own next-step, which proposed a second runbook elsewhere, now points here.
- **A structural bound was retracted after a reviewer's counter-example.** The cross-hour straddle was described as bounded by `min_gap_seconds`; H−1 books a stream's tail only when the *fleet*-dark window clears the threshold, and that is bounded above by the stream's own, so a thin pair straddles far wider. The real bound is the measured silence distribution — which, unlike the structural claim, degrades correctly when a thin pair joins the universe. Closed as a consciously accepted limitation at 0.045%, recorded at the clamp where whoever edits it will meet it.
- **Seven review rounds, none clean, and several found defects in the previous round's fixes** — a production abort under `set -u`, a false-green alert built while fixing another, a real dropped series on ops, an alert that could not fire, a runbook query returning nothing, and repeatedly tests asserting a property one level shallower than the one that mattered. The durable lesson: **a guard is unproven until the bug it names is constructed and seen to fail it.** Reading the assertion is not enough.

______________________________________________________________________

## 2026-07-30 — the zaccess internet access portal (spec/plan 00075)

- **A Linode bridgehead now fronts three home services with the home network accepting nothing inbound.** WireGuard `zaccess0` (bridgehead ↔ ops, `AllowedIPs /32` each way so a compromised VPS gets no LAN route), `systemd-socket-proxyd` relays (no NAT, no IP forwarding on either host), and a Caddy mTLS edge. **G1** (`ssh -p 20022 zhaow@zaccess.zhaow.me` → lands on `zcrypto-ops`) and **G3** (`https://nas.zaccess.zhaow.me` → the DSM console, through mTLS → tunnel → relay with full NAS-cert verification) are live and verified end-to-end. **G2** (agentboard web terminal onto the `zcrypto` tmux) shipped **spike-gated** by design — `access_ops_agentboard_live` defaults false so no converge can bring an unauthenticated terminal onto the weeks-long autonomous session on its own — and then went **live in this same iteration** once the owner ran the D13 non-destructiveness spike and flipped the flag; the browser terminal, and multi-client resize across a MacBook browser, the ops terminal, and an Ubuntu terminal, were all confirmed end-to-end.
- **The bridgehead is the fleet's fifth Ansible host, in one playbook** (`access_host`, joins `observed`), reusing the audited `base`/`hardening`/`firewall`/`fail2ban`/`chrony` roles; two new roles (`access`, `access_ops`) carry the payload. No Docker on the edge — Caddy and Alloy are native apt-pinned packages (the first non-containerized Alloy on the fleet, `host="zaccess"`), WireGuard is in-kernel, the relays are systemd. The `firewall` role gained an `extra-ports` seam proven byte-identical for the capture hosts by a golden-render test; the `hardening` role's stale-drop-in removal was parameterized.
- **The mTLS edge is the sole control (D2) and is proven fail-closed**: `require_and_verify` + an internal-CA `trust_pool` + a `verifier leaf` that pins the exact client leaf and rejects even the empty set. The negative test (no client cert) dies at the TLS handshake with no HTTP status; the positive test returns 200. Revocation is a leaf PEM's absence: the drill issued a throwaway leaf through the real vault-CA pipeline, saw it accepted, deleted it, converged, and saw it refused while the MacBook leaf still worked — proving the host-side orphan (the copy has no `--delete`) is inert because the re-rendered Caddyfile no longer lists it. Production Let's Encrypt certs for both vhosts via HTTP-01; renewal is Caddy-intrinsic and watched by a cert-expiry alert over both edge certs and the NAS's Sectigo cert (all three `zaccess_tls_not_after_seconds` targets in Cloud).
- **Observability from Phase 2, both-ended**: native Alloy ships node metrics; a probe timer on each end writes WireGuard handshake age and TLS `notAfter`, so the tunnel and the NAS cert are watched from bridgehead and ops alike. Alerts pushed and confirmed on the stack: `zcrypto-alloy-dark-zaccess`, `zaccess-disk-high`, `zaccess-tunnel-stale`, `zaccess-cert-expiring`. No hc.io dead-man for the bridgehead — Grafana Cloud nodata already covers "the bridgehead died", and it stays the capture/ops failure domain (D16).
- **Six real defects surfaced only at converge, none catchable from the repo, each fixed and re-reviewed** — the value of executing against live hosts: the minimal bridgehead has no `gpg` (→ `deb822_repository` for both apt repos), `deb822` doesn't refresh the cache (→ `update_cache`), Caddy (uid 997) couldn't traverse a `0750` pinned-leaves dir so the gate sat on the deb default `:80` (→ explicit `file` task + reload notify; `directory_mode` alone only fires on creation), an ansible-`trim_blocks` interaction collapsed the `verifier leaf` block (→ no Jinja whitespace-control), the edge-cert probe silently skipped under `pipefail` because `s_client` exits non-zero against a `require_and_verify` endpoint (→ `|| true`, let the emptiness guard decide), and the NAS presents a leaf-only chain for a `*.zhaow.pro` cert — not `*.zhaow.me` (a global find/replace had corrupted that one measured value) — so D7 validation needed the Sectigo intermediate pinned as the upstream trust anchor.
- **Two operational disciplines recorded** (`fleet.md`): `zaccess` converges need a single-identity SSH agent because `ssh_hardening` sets `MaxAuthTries 2` and `run.sh` offers the zaccess key last; and a Route53 subdomain-delegation trap — the apex address records must live in the delegated child zone, not beside the parent's `NS` record, which shadows them (this was the live symptom that blocked Phase 1 until fixed).
- **The tmux `window-size manual` pin crashes tmux 3.5a, and it cost two live-session losses during go-live.** D13 had proposed pinning `window-size manual` as a resize-conflict mitigation; on tmux 3.5a the server aborts on the next `new-session`, so agentboard creating its session killed the whole tmux server — twice, taking the autonomous `zcrypto` session with it. The managed `~/.tmux.conf` now carries only `set -g mouse on` + `set -g history-limit 10000` with a comment banning the pin, and `tests/test_infra_tmux_conf_no_window_size_manual.py` fails any regression. New hard rules landed in `fleet.md`: never run a test agentboard on the ops default tmux socket, never blanket `pkill -f agentboard` (it kills the live systemd unit), never leave a test agentboard's `PORT` unset (it defaults to 4040 and collides with the live one).
- **agentboard is node-version-independent, and the ops sshd was hardened for its new exposure.** The unit sources nvm and execs `agentboard` at start time rather than a converge-resolved absolute path, so a node upgrade via nvm needs only `systemctl restart` — no codebase change, no re-converge. A whole-branch cold review (Fable 5) also caught that the G1 relay had exposed ops `:22` to the internet while the box kept its home-LAN password-auth default; a `PasswordAuthentication no` drop-in (validated `sshd -t`, reloaded not restarted) closed it. A companion script (`infra/scripts/zaccess-extract-client-cert.sh`) extracts the vaulted client `.p12` + passphrase to `0600` files for browser import.

## 2026-07-30 — refine-rules round 2 (guidance corpus refinement)

- **Five lessons graduated from the memory inbox into always-loaded rules** on per-item owner rulings: guard-proving (a guard is unproven until its named defect is constructed and seen to trip it, failure mode read) and the stale-`.pyc` mutation-probe hazard into `agent-ops.md`; stage-by-explicit-path-one-kind into `commit-messages.md`; operational-rulings-land-on-the-operating-surface into `spec-plan-locations.md`; closeout-is-the-branch's-end into `iterations-history.md`. One candidate (exposure-change re-derives hardening) dropped on the owner's word.
- **Two new refinement principles**: P9 — a prohibition names a live temptation (a `Never` against something absent from the environment is template residue); P10 — a mechanized case needs no ambient prose (once a mechanism refuses at the point of use, the rule shrinks to a pointer, keeping only the wrong-recovery clause a guard cannot enforce).
- **The `staged-kind` pre-commit hook** (`infra/scripts/staged-kind-check.sh`) mechanizes the staging rule it shipped beside — claude-kind files never share a commit with another kind; proven against a constructed mixed staging at script level and through pre-commit, with claude-only/feat-mix/empty stagings passing, and independently re-proven by the cold review in a scratch repo.
- **Guard-class sweep over every prohibition in the corpus** (95 rule + 83 skill items): zero dead guards in the rules; three in the skills (the `glab` ban — not installed, GitHub-only remote, citing a rule that never made the claim; `cd src` residue; a GitLab comparison), all deleted. Eight guarded-rule shrinks landed where a mechanism already refuses (the two `config.alloy` drift-assert bullets, merge-pr's UNKNOWN triplication, dependabot's `main` bullet against verified branch protection, among others); nearly everything else earned a keep because the guard fires late/lossy or cannot block the tempting wrong recovery.
- **Staleness fixes**: the protected `config.alloy` bullets' consequence narrative (the drift assert now fails loudly — the silent exit-0 story predated PR #227), the panel out-of-scope-subtrees bullet had denied the tree scan `1668c610` added in the same commit as the sentence, the Coveralls caveat described a failure `fail-on-error: false` now prevents, CLAUDE.md's registration clause overstated an import form, and the fleet docs' image-repo header under-enumerated twice in one review cycle — it now states the invariant (match the pin row to the service, never to the repo; four services pin `zcrypto-capture` independently).
- **[[T0111]] registered and memo-queued** — mechanize `fleet-deploys.md`: 15 buildable guards in two waves (role asserts, then converge/panel-regenerate/postverify scripts) plus two M-sized asserts needing a design pass; each guard's landing PR shrinks its rule line in the same change, so the heaviest always-loaded rule shrinks as the guards land, never before.
- **Verification**: two cold reviews (Fable 5 on the round diff, Opus 4.8 on the extension), each finding real defects that were fixed and re-verified — including two of the round's own single-home relocation claims refuted with evidence; modal floor 76 → 78 with each removed `never` itemized; net always-loaded growth +846 bytes accepted by the owner.

## 2026-07-30 — the verification instruments, re-fitted (spec 00076, T0097 resolved)

- **The silence threshold is now fitted from real data, not guessed.** A week-of-data fit (340M rows) refuted T0097's own leading candidate — `10 × median` collapses to the 5 s floor because book updates burst same-millisecond, booking 2,061.7 s of phantom gap on a clean ETH/BTC week; the incumbent `p99.99 × 10` is the fitted winner, and `MIN_POOL = 5002` is pinned by a test that measures polars' nearest-interpolation behaviour rather than trusting a comment.
- **The hour-boundary head/tail test is now a single cross-hour timeline model** — `stream_timeline(segs, *, genesis_hour)` measures spacing across the hour boundary rather than treating each hour file independently, and annotates rather than books a stream's genesis hour — dissolving the false truncation this class of defect produced while the real T0036 restart-clobber signature still books above threshold.
- **The thin-stream false GREEN is closed by refusal, not by better measurement.** Any stream sparse enough to self-calibrate into blindness is now exactly a stream `MIN_POOL` declines to score (`UNMEASURED`). Proven against the real pre-fix module on the same fixture: OLD `thresh_s=2040.0, gap_s=4.0, 0.0556% PASS` vs NEW `n=2332, UNMEASURED, FAIL`.
- **The daily `archive verify-replay` is now windowed** (`ops_verify_replay_window_days`, 7 days) — the CLI's `--since`/`--pair` already existed and only the runner omitted them; proven with a synthetic bad hour that trips `checksum_present`: exits 1 unwindowed, 0 windowed. *(Reverted the same evening — see the next entry. The synthetic proof held; real data did not: a window cuts the chain-anchoring predecessor set, so 1,870 of 2,218 hours reported `anchored=False`. The variable no longer exists.)*
- **Acceptance run** (full 12-stream mirror, before/after merge-base `eef44196`): ETH/BTC 0.1320% → 0.0411% (genesis 535.7 s head no longer booked, now under the 0.1% bar), SOL/BTC 0.1332% → 0.0410% (genesis 535.3 s); truncations fleet-wide 72 → 60 (only the genesis false-RED removed — every EUR stream 7→6, both BTC-quoted 1→0), BTC/EUR's six surviving crossings (3276.0/1988.1/1890.3/270.7/126.7/83.4 s) the real restart-clobber events — the 2026-07-08 deploy-day sequence (3276.0 / 1988.1 / 1890.3 s) and the July-13 crash's 270.7 s signature; real outages preserved exactly (ETH/BTC's 256.2 s booked intra silence containing the 210.7 s event identical to the pre-implementation fit; BTC/EUR still books its 1550.8 s event inside 1805.9 s); fleet total 0.6131% → 0.4644%, verdict stays `*** FAIL ***` honestly — the EUR streams carry ~0.49% of REAL gap, and the change removed 30,370.1 s of fiction (12 genesis heads + the retired fixed 1s tail allowance), not real loss.
- **Closeout.** [[T0097]] → resolved and archived; one residual split before archiving, [[T0112]] (the derived threshold self-inflates on repeat outages — `MIN_POOL` protects against exactly one outage-scale interval, `n ≥ 10000k − 5000` for k of them; no production stream within three orders of magnitude of the k=2 regime — BTC/EUR 20,491 intervals of margin, LINK/EUR 10,508, ETH/BTC 2,197, SOL/BTC 898). `.claude/rules/fleet-deploys.md`'s genesis-carve-out sentence retired on the same branch (protected-file edit, owner sign-off) now that the instrument annotates the genesis hour itself. No trial registered and no budget spent — this iteration builds instrument, not evidence.

## 2026-07-31 — verify-replay pages on new breakage, not on a stale exit code — the second attempt (spec 00077)

- **The first attempt fixed the wrong layer.** Spec `00076` D7 windowed the daily `archive verify-replay` sweep with `--since` to stop one historical bad hour from paging its CRITICAL exit-code rule forever; it shipped 2026-07-30 and was reverted the same evening after failing 1,870 of 2,218 hours (all `anchored=False`) on real data, because a window cuts the chain-anchoring predecessor set — a fact `verify_replay`'s own docstring already stated before D7 was written. The real defect was never the sweep's *scope*, only what the alert *meant*: `ops_verify_replay_exit_code > 0` cannot distinguish a known, already-triaged bad hour from a genuinely new one, so any fix that left that meaning in place — including a windowing fix that had worked — would still have paged forever on the first bad hour it ever saw.
- **Three separate paging channels existed on that one finding, not one, and only the metric rule was obvious.** The healthchecks.io dead-man's ping was gated on `rc`, so once any hour ever failed `rc` stayed 1 forever and the ping — meant to prove the sweep was alive at all — was withheld forever; and the CLI logged every failed hour at ERROR on every run, which the ops ERROR-logs catch-all rule paged on nightly regardless of the exit-code rule's fate. Both of those channels would have kept paging forever even if D7's windowing had worked, since neither reacts to the sweep's scope — each needed its own, independent fix.
- **What shipped, across all three channels.** A failed hour now logs at WARNING instead of ERROR (a finding about data is not a fault in the program); the runner publishes `ops_verify_replay_failed_hours` (carried forward, never a sentinel, when a run breaks), `ops_verify_replay_hours_total`, and `ops_verify_replay_run_ok` on every run; the dead-man ping and `last_success_timestamp` now gate on `run_ok` — the ping on `run_ok` alone, and `last_success_timestamp` on `rc == 0` **and** `run_ok == 1`, since a sweep that never ran cannot have been clean; and the single `Ops · verify-replay non-zero exit` rule is replaced by two — `Ops · verify-replay NEW hours stopped replaying` (`delta(ops_verify_replay_failed_hours[25h]) > 0`) and `Ops · verify-replay run broken` (`ops_verify_replay_run_ok < 1`, `for: 15m`). Because the WARNING-level change touches `cli/`, this deploys as an image rebuild plus an ops re-pin rather than a template-only edit, which puts the liquidations-compose re-pin question (the role never restarts that compose on an `ops_image_digest` move) on the attended deploy tail.
- **A fourth adjacent fact was caught before shipping, not after.** Deleting a rule from `alerts.yaml` does not retire it — `grafana-push.sh` upserts and never deletes, and its own comment says a rule removed from the file "keeps evaluating and emailing forever." Retiring the old exit-code rule therefore needs the attended `GRAFANA_PRUNE=1` step; without it this iteration would have shipped with the very rule it exists to retire still paging.
- **Every task produced at least one guard that could not fail — eight in total, every one caught by review rather than by the suite, and two of them minted by the fix for a previous one.** Task 1's WARNING-level test passed only because two unrelated CLI-invoking tests ran earlier in the same file (`caplog` misses a logger that becomes non-propagating mid-test, and the CLI's root callback only flips `propagate=False` on its first invocation) — fixed by attaching `caplog.handler` to the logger directly, removing the ordering dependency. Task 2's ping-gate assertion matched the literal string `run_ok` in the surrounding comment rather than in the gate itself, so deleting the gate entirely still left all 11 template tests green — fixed by asserting the exact gate line. Task 3's `run-broken` alert summary read "unlike the rule above," a pointer that resolves to nothing on a phone in Slack with no sibling alert in view — rewritten to stand alone. The final review added three more: the parse test hardcoded its own input, so a CLI-side field rename shipped green; no committed test executed the carry-forward block, so collapsing it to a `0` sentinel passed all eleven; and the `last_success` gate's `run_ok` half was uncovered, so dropping it passed all twelve. Then the loop turned on itself twice. Replacing the hardcoded input with a whole-file substring check re-opened the same hole one level up — the old literal left in a comment satisfied it — and replacing *that* with an executable `CliRunner` check silently dropped the `hours=` field coverage the textual version had, so renaming that field passed all 28 tests while `hours_total` would have gone empty in production. The pattern is one assertion shape — a match window wider than the thing under test, satisfied by surrounding prose, or a branch nothing executes — and the sharpest lesson is that a fix for it is itself a prime site for the next instance. Every one of the seven *guards* was found by constructing the defect and reading which assertion fired; the eighth, Task 3's alert summary, had no assertion to fire and was caught only by reading the text cold as an operator receives it.
- **Deploy is attended and not run by this branch.** The plan's ordered tail (build → pull the digest on the host → record it in `fleet-pins.md` → converge `--limit zcrypto-ops` → decide the liquidations roll explicitly → push rules → **verify at the first tick, reading `failed_hours`'s value** → only then `GRAFANA_PRUNE=1` → confirm the old uid 404s) was the owner's checklist; **it ran 2026-07-31/08-01 and the outcome is recorded in `docs/reference/fleet-pins.md`**, including the pre-flight find that ops' Alloy compose was pinning the ops image digest as `grafana/alloy`. The prune is last on purpose: it is irreversible, and `delta()` is blind to breakage already present in a series' first sample, so pruning before the replacement is proven would retire the only rule that could have caught a bad hour born in that window.

## 2026-08-01 — verify-replay certifies the whole archive by replaying almost none of it (spec 00078, T0114 resolved)

- **The nightly canonical sweep is incremental, and its runtime no longer scales with the archive.** `zcrypto archive verify-replay --state-dir` caches each hour's raw verdict facts against the sha256 of the bytes **actually replayed** — not the sidecar's claim, since both writers publish sidecar-then-final and a crash mid-mint leaves a new sidecar over old bytes — and serves unchanged hours from one atomically-replaced `checkpoint.parquet`, ops-local at `/var/lib/zcrypto-ops/verify-replay-state/`, outside every NAS rrsync pin, with `/data` still mounted `:ro`. Steady state is the day's ~288 new hours (~23 min) rather than the whole archive (6,000 hours at 4.80–4.82 s/hour measured twice end to end = 8 h 00 m as of 2026-08-01, +288/day), so both of [[T0114]]'s dated cliffs are dissolved rather than pushed out: journald's `RateLimitBurst` (~2026-08-14 — per-run output collapses from one line per hour to four) and runtime meeting the 24 h cadence (~2026-09-11). Hours never seen before are always replayed; hours whose bytes changed drain oldest-first under a 7,200 s budget with the remainder announced as `pending`; a cached *failure* is never trusted, so a transient `ro,soft` NFS EIO still heals the next night instead of freezing into a permanently-failing hour. Three conditions exit 2 with the summary withheld so `run_ok=0` pages rather than the night reading green — an unwritable state dir, an enumeration that would evict more than a tenth of the checkpointed hours (an unmounted mirror, not a real shrink), and a sampled audit that caught the cache lying.
- **The property the whole design rests on was found at review, before a line of code existed.** The checkpoint stores only *raw* per-hour facts and the chain verdict is recomputed every run over cached and freshly-replayed rows alike. Caching the verdict instead would make anchoring **irrevocable**: `anchored = raw OR (contiguous AND prev_ok)` is a fold that can only widen, so an hour cached `anchored=True` stays true forever after the reconciler later rewrites its predecessor into a failure — and every successor stays green across a broken chain while `delta(failed_hours)` reads 0. A cold Fable review of the first draft caught it in the spec, along with four leaks in the invalidation argument. It is the same class of defect spec `00076` D7 shipped and reverted the same evening; the only difference is where it was caught, and here it cost a review round instead of a production night. Mutation confirms the pin bites and that it is a *single* pin: writing the chain verdict into the checkpoint rows makes an hour stay anchored after its predecessor breaks, and only the dedicated test catches it — the field-for-field equivalence test passes on a healthy tree, because there everything folds anchored anyway.
- **A sustainability reversal: the environment is recorded, it does not invalidate.** The first draft put `polars.__version__` in the invalidation key. Dependabot bumps polars roughly monthly (#218, merged 2026-07-28) while a full drain takes ~74 nights at year-one archive size, so the instrument would have sat permanently mid-drain, mixed-version, `pending` never reaching zero — the exact pathology at exactly the horizon this spec exists for. `polars_version` and `depth` are now recorded per row for forensics only, and environment drift is caught instead by the nightly sampled audit: 25 uniformly random **cache-trusted** hours re-replayed under current code and compared field-for-field, byte hash included. Constant cost, and strictly better detection than a year-long drain — it also covers overlay bit rot the NAS's hourly re-hash cannot see, checkpoint corruption, and a stale-predicate bug. The audit deliberately never samples *pending* rows: those are known-stale by construction, so auditing them would mismatch with certainty and false-page through every night of a legitimate drain.
- **The backlog alert's `for:` was wrong twice, and it is arithmetic rather than taste.** `pending` is a persistent textfile gauge scraped continuously between the daily runs, so on a *healthy* drain the condition (`pending > 0` **and** `delta(pending[26h]) >= 0`) goes true at the bump night's publish and cannot go false until the window's left edge passes it — a true run of exactly `max(24 h, window)` = 26.00 h, however much progress night two made. `for` must therefore **strictly** exceed that: `for: 25h` — the spec's own first number, faithfully implemented — fires on a healthy drain at the same instant it fires on a stuck one, and even `for: 26h` trips on the 26.00 h run, because equality is failure and not margin. Shipped `for: 27h` over `delta(…[26h])`, re-derived independently by two reviewers against the shipped values and pinned by a test that reads the window from the rule's own selector and `for` from the rule rather than from literals. The spec was corrected in place, since the wrong pairing had already reached operator-facing runbook prose. Stated precisely, because a looser version of this claim is easy to carry: a stall on the **first** drain night — the backlog never starts shrinking — holds true for 48 h and pages; a single stalled night **mid-drain**, after real progress, holds only 22 h and stays quiet; two consecutive mid-drain stalls page. A persistently *broken* run also carries `pending` forward unchanged, which is "not shrinking" by this arithmetic, so the runbook entry's first step is to read `run_ok`.
- **Guards that could not fail, again — ten across the six implementation tasks, every one caught by review or by the implementer rather than by the suite.** The shape is always the same: the suite stays green under exactly the defect the test or guard is named for. The plan's own atomicity test passed against a deliberately broken implementation. The plan's own audit test doctored `ts_ordered=False`, which makes that row a *cached failure* — so it drained and was never sampled, and the test could not have exercised the audit at all. The returned-results half of the audit's self-heal had zero coverage and its deletion survived the entire suite: under that bug a run reports mismatches while every returned hour reads green, so `failed_hours` counts 0 over a rotted archive and the fold carries the lie into every successor. The alert's firing direction was unpinned in **both** directions — flipping the threshold to `lt` makes the rule unable to fire at all, and dropping the delta half makes it page on any nonzero backlog, and both survived. The sharpest was a test literally named `test_only_the_checkpoint_dir_is_writable` under which mounting `/nas` `:rw` survived. Two of the ten were in the plan's own prescribed tests, which is the durable half of the lesson: **a plan is a prime site for this defect, not a defence against it.** One harness finding earns the same weight — a mutation anchored on a string that occurs twice rewrote the wrong call site and reported a *false surviving* mutation, so assert the anchor is unique before substituting; and assertion **order** decides what a test proves, since the natural order let the precedence regression trip the exit-code assertion first and short-circuit before the assertion that mattered.
- **Deploy is an attended tail this branch does not run.** Ordered: rebuild the ops image → pull the digest **on the host** (every runner is `--pull never`) → record it in `docs/reference/fleet-pins.md` *before* converging, since `ops_image_digest` has no repo default and that row is the only rollback operand → converge `--limit zcrypto-ops`, with **no** `ops_alloy_digest` (`config.alloy` is untouched) and the liquidations-compose roll decided explicitly, preferring to roll → push rules, **additive this time — nothing is retired, so no prune** → verify by value at the first tick: the census line present, `reused ≈ 0` on night one and `≈ hours_total − 288` on night two, `pending` draining, `failed_hours` unchanged, `00077`'s two rules still healthy. The first converge also creates the state dir. **Rollback is the digest and the runner template together**: the new template passes `--state-dir /state`, which the prior image's CLI rejects, so rolling back the digest alone yields no summary, `run_ok=0`, and a dark dead-man every night. The checkpoint dir is inert under the old image and safely deletable.
- **Closeout.** [[T0114]] resolved and archived, all three sub-items closed: checkpointing built (in a different shape than the topic proposed — there is no cross-hour `OrderBook` state to persist, only a pure fold whose carry is `(prev_hour, prev_ok)`), windowing **decided against permanently** rather than deferred (the runtime motive is gone and a window still cuts the chain-anchoring predecessor set, so `--since`/`--pair` stay ad-hoc tools and `--state-dir` refuses to combine with them), and the duration/hour-count metrics shipped as the run's census — `replayed`/`reused`/`pending`/`duration_seconds`/`audit_mismatches`, the last deliberately the one series **not** gated on `run_ok`, since an audit mismatch is the only run where it is nonzero and is exactly the run whose summary is withheld. Spec `00078` D11 corrected in place for shipped drift (the census ships seven fields, not six; five new series, not four). One plan-vs-code deviation, reviewer-approved and recorded here so a later diff does not read as drift: the engine assembles → saves → chain-folds where the plan said assemble → fold → save — externally identical and strictly safer for the property above, since the save happens before the fold's output exists. No dataset introduced (the checkpoint is ops-local operational state, not a research input) and no decisions-log entry owed — this iteration's decisions are engineering, not subject-matter research.

## 2026-08-02 — the exit-bar instrument refuses a contaminated tail instead of trusting it (spec 00079, T0112 resolved)

- **`continuity.py` now judges the tail it calibrates on, not just its size.** `MIN_POOL = 5002` protects against exactly **one** outage-scale interval; from two similar outages upward the pool clears the bound while `p99.99` lands *on* an outage, the derived threshold inflates to 10× the outage, and the instrument books 0.0 s over genuinely missing data — the false GREEN the script exists to prevent, arriving from repeat outages instead of from a small sample. A measured stream must now also satisfy **both** `p99.99/p99.9 < 10` and `p99.9/p99 < 10` (`TAIL_RATIO_CUT`), denominators floored at `RATIO_FLOOR_S = 0.5` — which is `5.0 / 10`, the spacing scale below which the existing threshold floor already declares steepness irrelevant, so an ultra-bursty pool with `p99.9 = 0` from same-millisecond bursts is not refused for being fast. Failing either ratio is `UNMEASURED` + exit-bar FAIL, exactly like the `MIN_POOL` refusal, because a contaminated threshold's output *is* the lie and printing it with a caveat would print the lie. Two chained decades because one does not span the range: the first catches `p99.99` landing on an outage, and once the outage count reaches ~0.001·n `p99.9` is contaminated too and reads ≈1, from where only the second ratio still spans the cliff. The cut is 10.0 because that is the per-decade quantile ratio of a Pareto tail at α = 1 — the infinite-mean boundary no physical spacing distribution crosses — which makes it one number, the same in both decades, sayable at 3 a.m.: *the tail must not steepen more than 10× across a decade of quantiles*.
- **The founding defect was reproduced against unmodified code before the fix existed, and the reproduction identified the cause more precisely than the topic had.** The carved k=2 fixture run through the pre-change module gives `n = 11,332`, `thresh_s = 2004.0`, `0.0 s` of intra-hour silence booked, `0.0000%`, and `EXIT BAR: PASS` — over 401.4 s of genuinely missing data. The k=1 control on the *same geometry* derives `thresh_s = 6.0`, books 200.4 s and FAILs. So the **second** outage is what breaks the instrument, not the fixture's shape; under the new code the k=2 fixture reports `UNMEASURED` and FAILs.
- **The `tail` depth column ships as transparency, and this iteration is why nobody should promote it into a gate.** It prints how many pooled intervals reach `p99.99` — how many data points the threshold rests on. It is provably **not** a contamination detector, measured rather than argued: at n = 11,389 a clean pool and one carrying two 200 s outages both have depth 2, because the count at or above `p99.99` is a deterministic function of n (≈ the tolerated k, plus one) and not of contamination. That equality is pinned as a test, so promoting depth into a gate means first deleting the test that disproves it. On a tie-free pool depth is exactly `round(0.0001·(n−1)) + 1`; a large depth on a tie-heavy pool is a tie artifact — a fixture reading 11,999 drops to 2 under 10 ms of jitter. **Two different populations, both correct, not to be conflated:** the cut was fitted on a **48-hour sample** of the 12 production streams (n = 240k–3.9M, depth 25–390); the full archive is a different population (n = 11.7M–220.9M, depth 1,167–22,087).
- **The cold review corrected the spec's own false-positive evidence before a line of code was written.** The margin table's "legitimate synthetics stay ≤ 4.5" was single-seed luck: over 200 seeds, pareto α=1.1 crosses the cut on 52 draws (26 %) and lognormal σ=3 on 90 (45 %) — exactly what the theory predicts, since pareto α=1.1's per-decade ratio is 10^(1/1.1) ≈ 8.1, brushing a cut of 10 by construction. Those families are parked *at* the boundary the cut is defined by, so they are the cut's definition rather than evidence against it; the load-bearing false-positive evidence is the **real streams** at 1.05–1.96, a 5× margin, against contamination at 88.8–200. The two pareto/lognormal fixtures that stayed are pinned to verified seeds — an unpinned seed there fails healthy code roughly one draw in three — and at ratios 4.93/6.65 those pins sit between the stable synthetics (~2.3) and the cut, so they would also catch the cut being quietly lowered to ~5–6.
- **Validation is split by what each data source can prove, which is the answer to "why synthetic data at all".** The reachable real path — a narrow `--since` window on a slow stream — cannot summon two genuine outages inside a 2-hour window on demand, so **synthetic fixtures construct the true positives** (the k=2 flip above, plus a k ≈ 0.002·n case that passes the first ratio and is caught only by the second, so removing the second ratio fails exactly that test). **Real data proves the absence of false positives**, which no fixture can. Neither source alone is a validation.
- **Acceptance on the full production archive: verdict-neutral, line for line.** Both the new and the shipped script ran through the **same** pinned ops image against the NAS archive read-only, so only the script differed. All 20 output lines are identical once the 7-character `tail` slot is removed — 12 streams measured in both, **zero** `UNMEASURED` rows, **zero** refusal notes, every verdict column (`thresh_s`, `gap_s`, `covered_s`, `gap%`, TOTAL, EXIT BAR) unchanged. Real-archive figures: n from 11,659,092 (SOL/BTC) to 220,858,582 (BTC/EUR), tail depth 1,167–22,087, `thresh_s` 8.8–40.4. The run shows `EXIT BAR *** FAIL ***` at 0.4122 % in **both** versions — that is the pre-existing genesis/truncation backlog spec `00076` left honestly on screen, unrelated to this change; the proof that this change is verdict-neutral is precisely that both runs agree line for line. Task 2's review had already made the same argument structurally, rendering eight tree shapes through both revisions and showing every line byte-identical after deleting exactly the slot `[43:50]`, which doubles as the alignment proof across all row types and both separators (`len(header) == len(rule) == 95`).
- **Two more guards that could not fail, both found by review rather than by the suite.** A mutant that misattributes **every** under-bound refusal as a steepened tail — the exact defect the two distinguishable refusal reasons exist to prevent — survived all 25 of the first task's tests, because nothing asserted the under-bound note was *present*. And deleting the empty-pool guard (`tail_depth(...) if n else ""`) passed all 38 tests, yet an empty pool makes the depth computation raise `TypeError` — on a **reachable** path: `n == 0` comes from a one-row hour segment, which is the T0036 restart-clobber signature this instrument exists to measure, so the exit-bar instrument would have died with a traceback on exactly the input it is for. Shipped code was correct in that second case; the gap was coverage, and both are pinned now. A third, smaller instance was created by the diff itself: the test helper's `field in line` column lookup began matching the new refusal note as well as the header (both contain "tail"), so with the column deleted it returned `''` and **passed spuriously** — anchored to the header row, the same probe now raises.
- **The wording defect this iteration created, and fixed.** Hoisting the refusal note out of `if not totals:` — so it prints whenever *any* stream is refused rather than only when nothing at all was measurable — made its own prefix false: `no measurable segments: ` printed twice directly above a TOTAL row aggregating a measured stream. It is now `unmeasured: `, the same word the table prints in those rows' `thresh_s` cell, so the note maps onto its rows at a glance. Deliberately **not** `unmeasured streams: `, which would collide with a real exit-bar-isolation assertion requiring a canonical (`--overlay`) report never to emit that substring. The hoist itself was the point: in production a contaminated stream almost always sits beside measured ones, and that is exactly where its reason is least guessable — a huge `n` beside `UNMEASURED` reads as "not the size bound" only to someone who knows `MIN_POOL`.
- **No deploy is owed and no dataset was introduced.** `infra/scripts/continuity.py` is run ad hoc from a repo checkout against a **pulled** copy of the archive (never the live dir) — it ships in no image and is driven by no timer — so the change is live for the next capture-deploy verification with nothing to converge. `fleet-deploys.md` and the rollout skill name the script but no column layout, so no rule edit is owed either.
