Disarmed, nothing is subscribed, so the adapter's 10 s idle timer measured the absence of data it was never sent and reconnected every 14.8 s — 40 attempts per rolling 10 minutes, 27 % of the per-IP Cloudflare budget (~150, 10-minute ban) that the engine host shares with the L2 capture primary and with its own REST order path. The heartbeat, unchanged at 30 s, still catches a dead peer at ≤ 90 s and keeps Kraken's ~60 s inactivity close at bay.
- **The value is pinned as the literal, because `None` means 10000.** An interface pin measures both readings on the pinned wheel; the builder test asserts `== 0` and was proven to refuse `None`.
- **The socket's behaviour is measured, not asserted — and offline.** A loopback venue (stub REST plus a WebSocket peer scripted three ways) runs a real data node in CI and separates four behaviours on one fixture: the adapter default reproduces the reconnect loop, the shipped `0` stops it, a peer that really closes still produces exactly one reconnect, and a raw peer that answers no ping is still caught by the heartbeat at three intervals. The last two are what let "zero reconnects" be read as health rather than as inert machinery. One venue-shaped row stays opt-in for the property loopback cannot show: Kraken closes an inactive socket at ~60 s, and a local peer never does.
- **The decision was challenged before it shipped, and the option space is recorded.** Fourteen options across four lenses, every claim refuted adversarially: every sentinel-subscription form is equivalent on the stakes and re-arms the trigger; every other knob is A in disguise or not viable; dropping either client breaks the boundary concordance. B stays available on its own armed evidence, re-scoped as an executor change. Two of the topic's own claims fell — the mid-order quote-delay story, and a 1 s backoff floor that was the successful-reconnect step, not failure pacing.
- **The cold review caught a runbook instruction that would have failed in the outage it was written for.** `docker stop zcrypto-engine` does not stop the engine: the unit is an attached `docker compose up` with `Restart=always`, so systemd restarts it ten seconds later and the retry storm resumes. The section names `systemctl stop` and says why the other form is wrong, in the same sentence.
- **What A does not fix is recorded where it will be read, not deferred.** The two-socket outage loop has no adapter knob — the only lever is upstream after the pin moves — and Rust-side socket lines never reach Loki. Both live in spec `00101` D7, in the runbook section, and in [[T0155]]'s body, so the topic's eventual closer sees them. No new topic, per the standing rule.
- **The converge is still owed.** [[T0155]] stays `partial`; it closes at the next attended, canary-gated engine converge — engine-only tags, and gated on the new image having baked as capture on the secondary first — verified by the idle-timeout count reading 0 at one hour and at 24 h.

## 2026-08-27 — iter-147: the external-order observer's two halves are joined against a real venue order, and the alert that step owed is declined ([[T0152]] resolved)

- **The join is PROVEN, by a single owner-placed order rather than by more waiting.** Spec `00100` D2's `ExternalOrderObserver` replaced a mechanism v2 deleted, and it had only ever been demonstrated in halves — the publish leg established from source, the delivery leg in a backtest — with nothing venue-sourced having traversed both. With the engine disarmed, one post-only limit placed outside the engine on ETH/EUR and later cancelled moved `zcrypto_exec_external_events_total{disposition="unmatched"}` twice; the counter carries only a `disposition` label, so what the events WERE is read back by event class from the executor's own shipped log line in Loki, both entries naming the same client order id. Publish → observer → counted → dropped holds for a lifecycle event and for that order's terminal, with controls that separate the result from a counter reset and from a dead scrape. The Cache-polling fallback the topic carried is not needed, and would have been new recurring behaviour to build rather than a rider on the executor's existing tick.
- **Only a window containing a known external order could ever have decided it.** Both disposition label sets are registered eagerly at engine start, so the series exists from boot whether or not the observer is reached — a reading of 0 cannot distinguish a wired observer with nothing to report from one that is never called. That is the machinery behind the silent failure the topic named, and it is why the disarmed soak could not have answered this on its own.
- **The healthy-boot baseline that had to precede the measurement is 0**, recovered from the metric's own history with PromQL `offset` rather than from a live before/after pair, cross-checking `process_start_time_seconds` at every point so a counter reset could not be read as a delta. The caveat travels with the number instead of being dropped: both boots were on a flat account, which is every boot before rung 1 and none after it, so the first disarmed boot carrying live orders or positions is a reading still owed — now owned by `infra/runbooks/engine.md`'s pre-probe step 4 rather than by a topic that is closing.
- **The `matched` disposition gets an owner it did not have.** A four-surface sweep found it thoroughly tested — including a differential control identical but for the `client_order_id` — and registered NOWHERE: the rung-1 exit bar names no external-order event, no alert rule covers the family, and the nearest structural owner — the `restart-reduce-only` ops drill named in the rung-3 go/no-go — sits in a topic that has no restart drill at all. It is registered in [[T0018]] as a one-off reading taken by VALUE, never by presence, because if the venue does not echo `cl_ord_id` the event falls to `unmatched` and both the working and the broken world read 0. The trigger is echoed in T0018's index bullet, which is what the pre-probe blocker sweep actually reads.
- **The alert the runbook ordered is DECLINED rather than left owed, and the ruling lands on the operating surfaces in the same change.** Pre-probe step 4 previously told the operator to author it once a baseline existed; it now records the decision and keeps item 1's future-version reading. The full reasoning — and the one change that would make a rule viable — sits in `tests/test_infra_alert_rules.py`'s `NOT_A_FAULT_SIGNAL` entry for the metric, where the next person to ask will read it before re-deriving it. What is declined is paging, not watching.
- **A plan-order deviation the branch surfaced is recorded rather than smoothed.** [[T0085]]'s bump sub-item had the engine converge sequenced AFTER the attended OS/apt upgrade + reboot round, so it would land on already-patched hosts; the v2 converge in fact landed 2026-08-26, before that round, which is still pending. The consequence is recorded with the deviation and points at the reboot bullet, whose existing constraint already covers it: that round will now restart a RUNNING v2 engine on the primary, which makes its inter-cycle-gap constraint load-bearing rather than routine. The sub-item, its frontmatter `ripe_when` — the surface a ripeness sweep actually reads — and the stale mirrors of the discharged owed-check count in the adapter-verification record move together, and the completed leg moves to `## Done so far` rather than being re-worded in place under `## Suggested next steps`.
- **[[T0152]] is resolved and archived carrying no live deferred sub-item**, both of its readings re-homed to surfaces that outlive it: the live-orders-boot baseline on the runbook's pre-probe step 4, the `matched` reading in [[T0018]].

## 2026-08-28 — iter-148: the reconcile ledger scan is measured, made cheaper, and given the monitors that will say when it needs renovating ([[T0044]] resolved)

- **The topic's own trigger could never have fired, which is the finding that reshaped the work.** [[T0044]] parked "re-measure when the ledger exceeds 250,000 records" — against a ledger holding roughly a hundred records and growing at about 1.2 a day. The measured cliffs are elsewhere entirely: memory binds first at **~40M records**, wall-clock second at **~600M**, so the registered trigger sat two-and-a-half orders of magnitude below the nearer of the two and would have raised a false alarm for the rest of the project's life. The response is the user's two-leg framing rather than a re-tuned number: raise the bar now with reasonable effort, and build the monitors that say when re-measuring is actually owed.
- **The bar is raised where it was cheapest to raise — the loader stopped materialising the whole file.** `_load_ledger` read `read_text().splitlines()`, holding the file's bytes and the split list simultaneously; it now parses line by line off the handle. Peak RSS at 1M records falls **1428 → 1217 MiB**, both figures measured in one regime rather than paired across two. The return type stays a `list` deliberately and the reason is recorded beside it: the caller appends to it mid-cycle, reads its `len()` three times, and queries it per pair-hour, so a generator breaks at the first append.
- **The scan now publishes its own cost.** `zcrypto_reconcile_ledger_scan_seconds` times both halves — the load and the totals pass — and sums them. It is a **required** parameter on `_write_textfile`, not a defaulted one: a default would publish a healthy-looking `0` from any caller that forgot it, which is exactly the reading the two alert rules are thresholded on.
- **The benchmark is committed, not a scratch measurement.** `infra/scripts/bench-ledger-scan.py` synthesises ledger records against a **growing** key space — hours advance monotonically with pair × kind cycling inside — because the first version saturated at 168 distinct keys and understated the totals pass by roughly 1.8×; its docstring at that point claimed the opposite of what its code did. Its module docstring now orders the constraints memory first, time second.
- **The memory figure was published wrong three times before it was published right, and the mechanism is worth carrying forward.** `ru_maxrss` is a high-water mark **inherited verbatim across fork+exec**, so a child of a 1215 MiB parent reports 1215 MiB while its own peak is 15 MiB — which is how a "fresh subprocess" measurement produced 2273 MiB. One process walking several sizes inflates every row after the first, for the same reason. The honest figures come from `VmHWM` in `/proc/self/status`, read in a child that holds no other ledger and does its own timing.
- **Two bars, a panel, and a runbook section — the monitoring leg.** `zcrypto-reconcile-ledger-scan-slow` (warning, `gt 10`) and `zcrypto-reconcile-ledger-scan-critical` (critical, `gt 30`) both carry `noDataState: OK`, so neither pages before the metric has a first sample. Panel 207 "Ledger scan — cost and size" plots the cost against the record count; it was placed at a `gridPos` that collided byte-identically with panel 201 and now sits below it with the rows beneath shifted. `infra/runbooks/ops.md` gains `reconcile-ledger-scan-cost`, carrying a `### Retire when` so the section names its own end condition.
- **The runbook's operative step says MemAvailable, because MemFree would misread the host tenfold.** The step originally said "free memory"; on the ops host `MemFree` reads 4.20 GiB against `MemAvailable` 47.15 GiB, so an operator following it under a page would have judged a healthy host three times over-committed. `docs/reference/fleet.md`'s ops row now records both figures with the note that they differ by about 10× and which one is the operand.
- **The deploy sequence this iteration owes, in order.** `zcrypto_reconcile_ledger_scan_seconds` reads `(no series)` today because the metric ships with the ops image: (1) converge `zcrypto-ops` so the metric starts recording; (2) **then** `infra/scripts/grafana-push.sh` for panel 207 and the two rules — never before, since a rule pushed ahead of its metric's first record pages a spurious no-data alert; (3) verify by VALUE with `uv run python infra/scripts/grafana-query.py 'zcrypto_reconcile_ledger_scan_seconds'`, where `(no series)` reads FAIL and never a zero. Both rules' `noDataState: OK` means nothing pages in between.
- **The topic resolves on [[T0103]]'s precedent** — watch-a-signal-then-act is a runbook shape, not a parked topic — and the growth-rate projection is consciously dropped rather than deferred: basket, frequency, strategy and capital size each move the landscape more than any curve fitted to today's ledger would predict.

## 2026-08-28 — iter-149: the capture silence bars survive their re-derivation, and the claims around them stop being false ([[T0129]] resolved)

- **Both bars re-derived against the full 14 d the platform retains, and neither moves.** `count_over_time(...[14d])` = 20159/20158 of a possible 20160, so the window is complete, and `[30d]` returns 20202 on both hosts — the same ~14 days plus a few dozen boundary samples, never a fuller window. Over the 13 days containing no venue window the fleet-wide minimum peaks at **6.134482 s** and the binding per-pair reading at **14.160757 s**, so 120 and 300 sit ~20× above an envelope nothing natural approaches.
- **The topic's own premise was the defect, and correcting it is the durable outcome.** [[T0129]] waited on a base it believed retention had capped forever. That is true of the **gauge** and false of the **phenomenon**: the parquet archive holds the whole capture era at full resolution and `infra/scripts/gap_distribution.py` measures book silence from it — the instrument behind the repo's own 14.78 s figure from July ([[T0039]]). Neither number is basket-wide for today's twelve pairs (T0039 covered BTC/DOT/AVAX before the `/BTC` legs existed; the gauge's binding pair is now SOL/BTC), so the two **bracket** the envelope at ~14–15 s rather than pinning it. A future re-derivation starts at the archive.
- **"The tail grew" is withdrawn — the shorter base had censored the estimate.** The gauge did move 12.068981 → 14.160757, but that is the same statistic over a longer window and a maximum rises mechanically with window length. The reading is consistent with the archive rather than exceeding it, which is also why the per-pair margin was recorded as ~25× when against the binding figure it is ~20×.
- **The measurement that reframed the work: every firing either rule has ever had was a published venue window.** Read from Grafana's alert state history — a different store from the 14 d metric retention — the critical rule has **4** genuine firings and the per-pair rule **48**, all on 2026-08-06 and 2026-08-20, both hosts, both inside an announced "Kraken Website and API Maintenance" window (07:01–07:16Z, components including `WebSocket`). No unexplained crossing has ever been observed. [[T0145]] had identified that class on 2026-08-21; what had not happened is the correction reaching the four surfaces where the claim is *read* — "no natural cause produces it" / "cause unexplained" survived in `alerts.yaml`, spec `00084`'s D11 rows, panel 102's description and the operator-visible summary. All four are corrected, and the rules now open by telling the operator to read that calendar.
- **Both rules gained the runbook sections they never had**, despite being the fleet's highest-severity capture signal and the sole guard on the both-host stuck stream. `infra/runbooks/capture.md`'s venue-halt step 4 also stopped telling the operator that these firings are "not something to respond to separately" — the alarm-fatigue instruction the topic warned about, already in writing on the only operator surface that mentioned them.
- **`execErrState` goes `OK` on these two rules alone, and the argument is narrower than the noise.** Over 2026-08-05→08-28 they raised **264** execution-error instances against **52** genuine ones — every one Grafana Cloud failing to reach its own Prometheus — and the page was not merely noisy but **false**: it asserts every stream on a named host has been silent for minutes, which the query never ran to observe. Not the guarded-summary form used on `zcrypto-capture-venue-state-recurrence` (`2a899adb`), which removes the falseness but not the volume — the per-pair rule mints 24 instances per error where that one mints one or two. Coverage is measured rather than assumed: only **6 of the 22** rules in `zcrypto-capture` carry `for: 0s` and can fire on a hiccup that short (the other 16 absorb it, which is why `zcrypto-capture-venue-not-online` logged zero), and four of those six keep `Alerting`. The residual is stated rather than implied — a **rule-scoped** error on these two now pages nothing — and a dedicated datasource-health rule is **consciously dropped**, not parked: a datasource outage is already over-reported, so it would remove noise rather than add coverage.
- **The deploy this iteration owes, in order.** `infra/grafana/alerts.yaml` is inert until pushed, so live Grafana still evaluates the pre-branch file: (1) run `infra/scripts/grafana-push.sh` — an upsert of existing uids, so **no prune is owed** and `GRAFANA_PRUNE` stays unset; (2) verify **by value**, not by exit code — read `execErrState` back for both uids from `GET /api/prometheus/grafana/api/v1/rules` and confirm each summary carries its `Runbook:` line; (3) rollback is a re-push of the previous file, since nothing is superseded. Until (1), the operator-visible summaries and the two `execErrState` settings are repo-only — the runbook sections and the dashboard JSON are separate and land with their own surfaces.
- **[[T0085]] gained the index's missing third category as a registered item** — `docs/open-topics/README.md` has only *Research and development* and *Live trading preparation*, and neither fits a topic discovered while **running** the system. Registered rather than built, because the change is only correct if `.claude/skills/topic-ops/SKILL.md` moves with it: its placement step names exactly two categories and is what a filer actually reads, so a category living only in the index would be invisible at the moment a topic is filed.
- **The branch's own recurring defect was the very one it exists to kill, three times, and only adversarial review caught it.** A correction landing where a claim is WRITTEN and not where it is READ: the per-pair rule kept the whole pre-branch base paragraph — "THE BASE IS ONE WEEK", a live-tense pointer to a topic this branch resolves, and the withdrawn retention premise — while its sibling ninety lines above was fixed, because the script applying that edit aborted on a later anchor **before its write** and only the two later edits were re-run. Spec `00084`'s D11 is a two-row table and only one row was corrected. And the branch **introduced** a false claim of its own — "that event can never be re-measured", eleven lines below its own correction that retention caps the gauge and never the phenomenon — then repeated the pattern in the runbook, asserting "nothing goes unwatched" twelve lines above the residual saying a rule-scoped error pages nothing. Every edit now writes immediately rather than batching to a single write at the end.
- **Review discipline changed as a result, and the change is in `.claude/rules/spec-plan-locations.md` rather than in this entry.** Two rounds had both confirmed the substance in round one — thresholds, `for:`, `execErrState`, the coverage arithmetic — and then spent themselves on prose, each fix becoming prose the next round found. The dispatch caused it: an open-ended sweep returns a backlog whose fixes are more prose. A review dispatch now **ranks by blast radius** (what ships to production, what a paged operator acts on, what a number changes; everything else Minor by construction), **never asks for an open-ended sweep**, and is **capped at two rounds on one subject — but only once nothing above Minor remains**. That last bound was itself a review finding: as first written the cap would have let a Critical be found, fixed, and the fix commit ship unreviewed, which this branch is the live counterexample to. A fix commit takes its own mandatory pre-push review, never "round three". The narrow third dispatch that followed proved the prose-only commit inert *mechanically* — `yaml.safe_load(before) == yaml.safe_load(after)` — rather than by reading it.
- **The rule file was then condensed to instructions-only, and the condensation lost something a diff read would not have caught.** Reasoning survives only where an instruction would otherwise look wrong and get reverted — self-certification on predate-the-branch, nothing-in-the-gate-catches-it on spec immutability, invisible-at-execution-time on operating surfaces. Verified by extracting every binding statement from the pre-edit file **blind** and checking each against the result: two independent readers both found the ban on open-ended sweeps gone, because the prohibition had been buried inside the narration that was cut. It is back as a bare imperative. 4448 → 4382 bytes, and the modal-verb count rose 3 → 4 — smaller and stricter.

## 2026-08-28 — iter-150: the rollout gate closes at gate-close, memory becomes a fleet-wide routine, and every converge writes its own record

- **The diagnosis was measured, not argued, and it was narrower than "the bake is too long".** The designed gate — a strong-form prune, three full segment-rotation hours, abort signals clear — has always closed in ~4–12 h (03d4cf1b: secondary 08-22 22:00Z → primary 08-23 09:25Z; v2: 12:00Z → 16:39Z). The multi-day tail was entirely the RSS residual: hand-scheduled reads at T+24 h and T+50 h against the host's own predecessor at equal process age, which the next converge voided — so the reads became a lock on the fleet. Across 88 fleet-pins commits since July **no rollback was ever executed, no abort signal ever tripped, and no RSS read ever found a real leak**; the one reading the history calls "most useful" (08-02) nearly rolled back a healthy image on a cold-baseline comparison. `process_resident_memory_bytes` appeared in zero alert rules.
- **The owner's three rulings: leak detection leaves the converge and becomes a telemetry routine that runs regardless of converges; 70 % headroom; and a machine line per converge.** The gate is now hard-capped at its three events — gate-close is PASSED, nothing after it is owed to a rollout, and the next converge never waits on an open read. The skill lost its residual paragraph and every T+ reading band; the abort table's RSS-slope row became the headroom rule firing on the just-converged host; `fleet-pins.md`'s rows lost their "reads OWED at T+32.6 h and T+50 h" clauses.
- **Three rules, fleet-wide, in the `zcrypto-fleet` group — measured from the live label sets rather than assumed.** They cover every long-lived process the fleet scrapes: both capture daemons, the engine, the ops liquidations poller, and Alloy on all four hosts under `job="integrations/self"`, the label `exporter.self` metrics actually carry (the scrape blocks set no `job_name`, so the component-path guess would have selected nothing). `zcrypto-fleet-memory-headroom` reads RSS against the container limit — the one number that says how long until the OOM-kill — with literal-byte denominators (no cadvisor on any host) that a test pins to the ansible vars and the Alloy compose literals, so a limit moved in one place fails the test instead of silently re-scaling the ratio. `zcrypto-fleet-memory-leak` is the bake's own reading method as PromQL — hourly floors, 24 h apart — with a **provisional** 64 MiB bar, registered as such because no leak has ever been measured here. `zcrypto-fleet-daemon-restarted` reads `process_start_time_seconds`, the same fact as `RestartCount` from inside the process; it pages once per converge or Alloy bump as that action's own record, and any other firing is the OOM-kill or crash nothing else reports. None carries `for: 0s`, so the capture group still has exactly six rules a datasource hiccup can fire. Deliberately not covered, and said where it is read: the liquidations poller has no memory limit in its compose, so headroom has no ceiling to measure (leak and restart cover it); the NAS `archive-pull` container exposes no metrics at all.
- **The leak rule's first version claimed a property it did not have, and review caught it.** "Blind by construction for a day after a restart — no floor 24 h back to subtract" is false: `offset 24h` addresses the series by labels, which a restart does not change, so the subtraction reads the *predecessor* process's floor — a new image's larger working set would have paged as a leak during the bake, and at the 24 h anniversary a warm floor would have met the converge-time cold one, the exact cold-baseline read the deleted skill text forbade. The rule is now gated on process age > 30 h (the band plus the pending period) with `and on(host, job)`, and the test asserts the gate.
- **`converge.sh` records every real pass.** It no longer `exec`s `run.sh`; it runs it and appends one JSON line — target, tags, every `-e` operand, revision, dirty flag, rc — to `docs/reference/deploy-log.jsonl`, so the digest and timestamp a rollback needs are written by the pass that set them and never re-typed from memory. Best-effort and loud: a record that cannot land is printed for the operator, and the script returns the pass's rc — the pre-existing harness tests caught the first version turning a missing directory into a converge failure that did not happen. Preview-only runs and aborted confirms leave no line. `fleet-pins.md` stays the state file, re-trued *from* that line. Recording shape, now on the operating surfaces: one PR per rollout, two commits, merged within the day, nothing branched from it.
- **Two files are named for what they cover.** `capture-deploys.md` — every tier's converge, the alert-rule lifecycle, the ansible secrets hazards, capture a minority of its text — is `fleet-deploys.md`; `zcrypto-captures-rollout`, which rolls the one app image serving all four tiers to the capture hosts and the engine, is `zcrypto-rollout-image`, verb-object like `zcrypto-bump-alloy`. 85 files followed by sed. The sed also rewrote two archive link targets in the open-topics index whose files rightly kept their names — a topic's filename is its identity — and a new test walks every topic link in the index so the next mechanical rename cannot leave a dead pointer on the surface every review consults.
- **`zcrypto-bump-alloy` was checked against this branch and is not invalidated**; it gains the two facts that touch it — the deploy log records the digest it is passed, and the restart rule pages once per host on every Alloy recreate, the bump's own record.
- **The deploy this iteration owes, in order.** (1) `grafana-push.sh` — three new rules and the retitled panel 602, an upsert; the rules were never pushed under their earlier `zcrypto-capture-*` uids, so **no prune is owed**. (2) Verify by value: `uv run python infra/scripts/grafana-query.py 'process_resident_memory_bytes{job="integrations/self"}'` returns four hosts, and the three rules read `Normal` from `GET /api/prometheus/grafana/api/v1/rules`. (3) Expect `zcrypto-fleet-daemon-restarted` to stay quiet until the next converge, and the leak rule to be gated off for 30 h on any process younger than that. The runbook, skill, rule, script and pins changes take effect on merge.

## 2026-08-29 — iter-154: the decompose table keeps the whole-book limits' share (spec-less, [[T0121]] resolved)

- **A report that would have blamed the governor for the risk layer's work.** `zcrypto engine decompose` stepped `capped` straight to `final` and labelled the transition "fraction left by the volatility governor". But `final` is `multiplier * limited` by construction — `_check_stage_identity` enforces that exactly — so on any cycle where a §10 whole-book limit bound, the limits' share would have been charged to the governor's row. That is the report an operator opens to understand `zcrypto_engine_limit_bound_total`, so the counter would have named the event and the table would have misattributed it.
- **The value was already there.** `replay_stages` computed `limited`, passed it to the identity check, and discarded it. The change carries it rather than recomputing: a required field on `CycleStages`, `limited_gross`/`limited_ratio` in the payload, a `limited` column, and the summary split into `capped -> limited` and `limited -> final`.
- **Required, not defaulted.** A default would let a future construction omit the stage and fall back to a book that is only correct while nothing binds — the same shape as the defect being fixed.
- **Proven where the two shares differ.** Every pre-existing fixture has no limit binding, so `capped -> limited` is 1.000 and the merged label read correctly on all of them; a test built only on those would have passed before the change. The new fixture gives the limits a quarter of the book and the governor a half, pins each share to its own line, and asserts they are unequal so it cannot decay into a degenerate one. Under the old column the line printed the multiplier, 0.500, under the label `capped -> final`, crediting the governor with the limits' quarter as well.
- **The trigger was the finding.** T0121's `ripe_when` waited for `zcrypto_engine_limit_bound_total` to move off zero "or the table to be touched for any reason". The first half waits for the failure before fixing the thing that reports it — and the counter read 0 on `zcrypto` when this was checked on 2026-08-29, having read 0 since it deployed on 2026-08-19, so it may never fire. The second is not a condition anyone evaluates. The work was gated on nothing and took one commit; the validation that found this cost more than the fix.

## 2026-08-29 — iter-155: the sleeve-promotion path is ruled, then re-ruled when the code disproved it (spec-less, [[T0123]] partial)

- **§12 had no answer to "an ADOPT verdict happened — now what?"** Research continues after go-live under the same registry, so a family eventually supersedes the deployable, and nothing said which of the four gates that made it trustworthy must be re-earned. The ruling scopes re-entry by **what the change touches** rather than by a duration: always a new Phase-5 record, a builder-contract bump, Stage-6a concordance re-earned, and a head-to-head win over the **incumbent**; beyond that, class **A** (weights only) adds nothing, **B** (new legs) adds rung 1, **C** (changed execution semantics) adds rungs 1–2. The class is pre-registered before the evidence, like a trial, so it cannot become a post-hoc argument for the least work.
- **The first form of the ruling was wrong in both of its mechanisms, and a cold review caught it.** It claimed parity re-earns by replaying the new contract against the incumbent's journal — but `replay_stages` raises whenever rebuilt targets differ from the journaled ones, and that difference *is* the promotion. It cited `_check_stage_identity` as an exact-equality gate with no sample size, when that symbol's own docstring says it "structurally cannot catch" divergence from what the engine traded; the ratified Stage-6a gate is `compare_targets` at `tol=1e-6` over `_GATE_STREAK_DAYS = 14` — neither exact nor duration-free. Parity now re-earns on the candidate's **own** journal, produced disarmed beside the live record, at the full unshortened 14 days, which cost no calendar because they nest inside the band window.
- **"Cut over when the band passes" could never have greened.** The go/no-go band is defined on realized return *with its fills*; Stage 6a logs intended orders only; so `weekly_tracking` returns `insufficient-data` for a shadow book forever. The band therefore **splits**: the floor half (p95 per-cycle drift floor at the funded NAV, by `accum-replay`) is measurable from shadow cycles and quoted in the cut-over decision, and the realized half accumulates **after** cut-over on the new record's own fills — ≥3 complete ISO weeks, every class, with the tracking-error trip and the DD ladder armed. Cut-over re-enters at A 50 % of funded NAV, B and C at rung 1.
- **The objection that had rejected a step-down did not survive either.** It rested on the edge being the p95 floor *at the funded NAV*, but §12 already re-derives the band whenever the target series moves — so band evidence was never transferring across NAVs, and the decisions log records the partial overturn where the rejection stands rather than appending a correction beneath it.
- **What condemned the first form was convergence, not critique.** The review dispatched three designers who solved the promotion problem blind, without seeing the ruling. All three re-earned concordance on the new contract's own live cycles and none used replay-against-the-incumbent; all three sequenced the realized band after cut-over. Three independent solvers rejecting the central mechanism and agreeing on its replacement is a verdict on the mechanism.
- **Two corrections the review surfaced beside the ruling.** The record lineage is 33 → **43** → 44 → 47, not 33 → 44 → 47 — and 43 is why the contract bump is a rule, its construction having been recoverable only by a salvage from a workstation-local transcript ([[T0148]]). And the `ripe_when` written with the ruling waited on the topic's own parked work, which nothing else would ever build; it is re-cut to the ADOPT verdict the registry actually mints, so a future index review can evaluate it.
- **The remainder is one capability, deliberately undesigned.** The ruled path needs the candidate runnable beside the live record and scorable under its own contract; the engine selects neither today (the builder is imported directly, with no `builder_contract`/`contract_version` symbol). Its shape is left to the first real promotion rather than pre-designed against zero candidates.

## 2026-08-30 — iter-156: every alert gets a runbook, and a daily pass that reads the fleet — with a guard that took six rounds to stop lying (spec 00104, [[T0157]] partial)

- **51 of 83 alert rules could page with no runbook.** Each now resolves to a section under `infra/runbooks/` in one shape — 50 written here, the fifty-first an existing section whose rule had simply never carried the link — *What you are seeing* / *What it means* / *What to do* / *Retire when* — reached from the rule's own `Runbook:` annotation, with a test that goes red on any rule lacking a resolving link (it was red on exactly those 51 before the work, and is green now). Seven readers working in parallel found more than gaps: `gate-mismatch` is a per-run recount over the whole journal rather than a 24 h window; the scoring cache survives a restart and dies only on a recreate; the NAS journal channel logs no `failed=` at all, so its dead-man stays green while that channel alone is broken; and the desync rule's "single fire-and-forget resubscribe" is really a ladder, so 15 minutes stuck means post-ladder. The README's `gate-export` row was stale in a way the runbook work disproved, and is corrected here.
- **The pass has something to read.** `infra/scripts/ops_daily.py` reads alert state and history, Loki, the dead-men two ways, the fleet verdict and the window's deploys; exit 2 (a source it could not read) outranks exit 1, because a source that cannot be reached is a finding about that source and never a silent gap. The alert read was built on an invented `labels.__a_uid__` and the fixtures had been shaped to the invention — measured against the live API, the uid is a top-level field and no `__`-prefixed label exists, so every test would have passed while the pass matched no alert to any runbook.
- **`fired_in_window` was a copy of `firing_now`.** History was fetched and discarded, so an alert that fired at 02:00 and cleared at 03:00 — the pass's core case, the whole reason to read overnight — reached nothing. The frame's shape was then measured against the live API rather than assumed.
- **The tier classifier decides what an unattended agent may run against production, and six review rounds found an escape in it every time.** Rounds 1–3 broke a parser that admitted a head and then argued its arguments harmless: shell composition, then attached and combined flags, then the operator spellings `|&` and `&>` — `echo x |& rm -rf /var/lib/zcrypto-capture` was AUTONOMOUS *while naming the capture directory*, because the read verdict short-circuited the protected-object check. Beside it, `sort -o FILE` and `uniq IN OUT`, which write through an operand that is a filename **by position, with no flag at all**. That is what condemned the approach rather than the implementations: no flag allowlist can catch an argument that is a file because of where it sits.
- **So the question was inverted.** A command is autonomous only when it matches an enumerated shape — a literal head, the flags it may carry, how many operands of which class — and prepared otherwise. Fixed operand arity refuses the positional writes without needing to know they write; `sort` and `uniq` exist only as pipeline filters taking zero file operands; `awk`, `sed`, `xargs`, `tee` and `dd` are absent entirely. The tokeniser is exact rather than approximate: one scan tracking quote state, refusing each metacharacter that is *active where it stands*, which is what makes `grep -iE 'collector|error'` one stage and `--format '{{.State.Status}} {{.RestartCount}}'` one token.
- **Every escape after the rewrite was in a hand-written predicate, never in the table.** Round 4 found the two post-checks carried over unexamined: `curl -o` wrote any file (and sat on the read path, which returns before the protected-object veto), and `docker inspect --format '{{.Name}}{{json .}}'` printed a live trade key, because a Go template reaches the whole object through a bare root reference that mentions no selector at all. Round 5 found the secret veto added *by round 4* — a denylist of secret-looking names, walked past by `cat <dir>/*`, in the same commit whose docstring said the old checks "failed by allowlisting what they could name". Round 6 found `-e` consuming grep's pattern, leaving the first **file** at the operand the path check skips *as* the pattern. Composition, then flags, then positional operands, then which operand, then which root: each fix correct, the next layer down still wrong.
- **The corpus is the honest half of the guard.** A test walks every command the runbooks actually contain: no command carrying a destructive token may classify autonomous (0 of the 36 that carry one), and ≥70 % of the read-only diagnostics must (176 of 233, 75.5 %, reproducible from that test's own extraction) — so a narrowing that makes the pass refuse its own diagnostics goes red too. The number dipped once, at round 2, and has been flat since. But the corpus could not have caught a single one of the escapes above, because no runbook contains an attack; they are pinned fixtures now, and each fixture was mutation-proven to go red when its fix is reverted.
- **`prepared` means prepare it and stop** — and now also *not yet enumerated*, which the skill says explicitly, because the disposition differs: prepare the step, then add the shape on a fix branch, never widen the table in the moment to get the pass moving.
- **What this iteration did NOT prove.** The instrument has never run against the live stack, and the runbook summaries have not been pushed to Grafana: both need a vaulted credential plus an outbound call, which this session's permission gate refused. The first real pass is therefore [[T0157]]'s remaining sub-item and the spec's own acceptance test — archiving the topic before it would record a resolution that nothing demonstrated.

## 2026-08-30 — iter-157: the signals that were silently absent — reminders, log levels, and the descriptions no test can reach (spec 00107)

- **A reminder that never arrived, and no way to notice it had not.** [[T0103]]'s healable re-derivation was armed as a Slack message scheduled for 2026-08-27; `#zcrypto` read across that whole UTC day holds two messages, neither of them it. The incident is one lost message — [[T0105]]'s 2026-08-05 ping landed, so the mechanism works — but the structural fact is worse: the Slack MCP can *schedule* a message and offers no way to list or verify one, so an arming that failed to exist is undetectable from this side, and the archived topic asserted the opposite ("cannot be deleted through the API, so it will land in `#zcrypto` on the day"). D1's answer moves the load off the ping rather than re-arming it: `read_reminders()` is the daily pass's sixth read, computing due-ness every day from state it can actually reach — the refdata sweep from the last dated row under `## Re-confirmation log` in `docs/reference/kraken-snapshot-register.md` plus the monthly cadence, the healable re-derivation from whether its counter moved in the window. It reports and never blocks: an OWED line leaves the verdict `all-clear`, and only a source the pass could not READ is `unreadable` → exit 2. The first live run after the change printed `ok refdata sweep: due in 5 days (last sweep 2026-08-04)` and `ok healable re-derivation: counter unchanged in 24 h`, each carrying the runbook anchor it would send an operator to, and agreeing with the two counter values read independently before the branch (`increase` 0, `resets` 0). Exit 0 before, exit 0 after; the two readings differ structurally in exactly two places — the new `## Reminders` section, and the `descriptions: all 10 carry a resolving runbook link and no internal token` line the descriptions check adds under `## Dead-men`.
- **~1200 lines a day of WARNING that meant "a reconnect happened".** The three `dropping late event` / `dropping replayed event` sites in `cli/capture/segment_writer.py` log at INFO, on a measured basis rather than a taste for quiet: the volume arrives in bursts, two buckets in 24 h, and `increase(zcrypto_capture_reconnects_total[24h])` read **2** on `zcrypto` and **1** on `zcrypto-red` — matching the bursts one-for-one — while resubscribes, resubscribe errors and desyncs all read 0 and `increase(zcrypto_capture_gap_seconds_total[24h])` read 0 on both hosts. They record the expected consequence of a normal reconnect, and the scraped counter is a better handle than counting log lines ever was; a comment at each site says so, because the next editor's instinct on seeing "dropping" at INFO will be to raise it back.
- **`SegmentWriter` is shared, and the second consumer does lose something — named and accepted (D4), not deferred.** The two liquidations writers log the same `dropping replayed event` line, and there a drop is not reconnect noise: spec 00055's bucket watermark filters re-submissions at source, so a drop that still fires means the watermark regressed. The reconnect counter is capture's alone, and the rules over `host="ops"` select `ERROR|CRITICAL` or `level=~".+"`, so the daily pass's `## Logs` count was the only automated surface those lines had there. What holds the invariant instead is `tests/test_liquidations_coinalyze.py::test_poll_cycle_second_cycle_is_silent_no_dedup_drops`, which runs in CI on every PR — and re-levelling its capture to INFO is part of this change, not housekeeping beside it: captured at `logging.WARNING` it would have gone vacuous the moment the level dropped. Three existing captures moved WARNING→INFO. What is genuinely given up is detection of a **runtime-only** watermark regression that no code change caused; it costs no data (the writer's dedup and the late-event floor are untouched) and is still readable from raw INFO logs.
- **And the noise reduction has not happened yet — this iteration does not deliver it.** The pass read `zcrypto/capture WARNING: 601` before the branch and `601` after: the capture daemon runs from a container image nobody has rolled, so the demotion lives in the source and not on the hosts. It reaches them with the attended capture-image rollout [[T0037]]'s `ripe_when` already owes, which carries the digest; this branch owes no rollout and registers none, and the pass's `## Logs` count is the evidence of the gap until that one lands.
- **The healable reminder has three states, and the third is why it is not a subtraction.** Moved → the recount is owed and the runbook section named; unchanged → nothing is owed; **reset** → its own state, because the counter is re-emitted from the ledger's totals every cycle, so a correction or rebuild that lowers the total is a reset and `increase()` then reports the whole post-reset value as movement. That is exactly the hazard `zcrypto-reconcile-healable-gap-rate` guards with `resets()` over the same counter and window, and the reminder mirrors it: on a reset it names the reset, owes the ledger recount, and never quotes the number. Where the counter did move, the figure is quoted as the scraped, extrapolated approximation it is and never as the ledger's delta — the pass does not attempt the qualifying-day COUNT at all, because the runbook's own step 1 makes the ledger the arbiter and Grafana Cloud retains ~14 days while every event predates that window. Which is also what keeps the instrument pure-HTTP: an `ssh`-plus-`sudo` ledger read would have been its first host dependency and a new class of unreadable source, to answer a question the counter already answers.
- **The one operator-visible surface no repo test can reach.** All three healthchecks.io descriptions that predate 2026-08-30 carried internal tokens — `Phase-6`, `spec 00050`, `T0083` — on a surface read from a phone with nothing open; they survived because `operator-facing-text.md` is enforced by a test over git-tracked files and these are hand-written in a SaaS. `check_descriptions` makes two assertions per check over the ten the pass already fetches: at least one `Runbook: infra/runbooks/<file>#<anchor>` citation with **every** one of them resolving against a real `<a name=…>` tag in the file it names — the literal prefix is part of the shape, so a passing mention is not accepted as the link and a dead second link cannot hide behind a live first — and no internal token. The true positive is today's real ten, committed as `tests/fixtures/healthchecks_descriptions.json` carrying the fields name/tags/desc and nothing else (no ping or write URL reached the repo), so a check that refuses everything goes red in CI. It also answered the question the branch deliberately deferred to measurement rather than guessing: all ten spell the prefix exactly `Runbook: infra/runbooks/…`, all ten pass, and no live description is a finding of this pass — so the prefix-widening ruled in a task earlier was insurance, not a fix.
- **The owner's ruling, carried by the code and pinned by the tests: a description finding is a report line and a journal clause, never the exit code.** The verdict stays `all-clear` / exit 0 unless something else fires. The check detects and cannot repair — the descriptions live in a SaaS no repo change can touch — so an escalation would not clear when the branch merges, when CI goes green, or on any later pass; only when a human logs into healthchecks.io. Every day's entry would read `attention` until then and the all-clear entry that is the journal's whole product would be gone for the duration, and it would invert the severity ordering against D2, where an OWED sweep — real overdue work on externally-owned facts — leaves the exit code at 0. Visibility is therefore the entire guarantee, and one test asserts the finding line under `## Dead-men`, the clause in the journal paragraph and `exit_code == 0` together. The remedy the ruling owes is a step, not an alert: the daily-ops skill's new step 6 tells the pass to rewrite that check's description in healthchecks.io with the admin key (the read-only key the report reads with cannot write), send the description field alone so the check's schedule and grace period are untouched, and read it back.
- **Every guard mutation-probed through `infra/scripts/mutate-probe.sh` — all KILLED — and the harness proven able to say otherwise.** Per-site on the three drop lines; the two cadence clauses of `_a_month_after` (the December year rollover, the day clamp) and, in a fix round, the register parser's `#\d+` clause and its section gate, the gate's own `in_log = False` initializer closed by the final wave with a fixture carrying a dated row *above* the first heading; five on `read_reminders` (a counter that did not move read as movement, a reset read as movement so the false number reaches the report, first-row-wins, a section-blind parser reaching a 2099 decoy and answering "not due" from a table it should never have read, and due-today read as not-owed); the runbook-anchor rename the citation guard catches; the owed-reminder-escalated-into-the-attention-condition mutation; and six on the descriptions, including the plausible "fix" of escalating a finding to attention. The three drop-site verdicts are three per-site proofs rather than one joint proof recorded three times, because probing the held-hour test while mutating the `_admit` site returned **SURVIVED** — the negative control an always-KILLED harness cannot supply.
- **D6 — the beliefs that made the gaps invisible, corrected where they live.** [[T0103]]'s archived claim that the scheduled message "will land on the day" is re-trued to what was measured, and to the structural point that the arming cannot be verified from this side at all; [[T0037]]'s parked-residuals bullet, whose argument rested on the late-event drop being a bare WARNING the rollout skill documents as noise, now rests on it being expected reconnect replay by design — which is the argument *for* `zcrypto_capture_hour_finalized_early_total`, not against it. The `zcrypto-rollout-image` skill told an operator that post-start `dropping late event` lines are **resubscribe** replay and to read the reconnect counter; that counter increments only after a closed connection or a failed attempt, so a clean first connect leaves it at 0 and an operator converging a secondary would have read 0, concluded the benign explanation failed, and rolled back a healthy host. It now says restart replay, and that zero there is correct. The three operating surfaces that still named Slack as the trigger — `reference-data.md`'s opening and its `#refdata-sweep-due` section, `ops.md#healable-threshold-rederivation-due`, and the daily-ops skill's step 5 — now name the report's `## Reminders` section; the refdata-sweep skill gained the row shape `last_sweep_date` requires, down to the spacing, since the register is outside mdformat's reach; the journal README's example paragraph gained the reminders clause; and the memo's note was rewritten in place rather than corrected beneath itself. `archive/T0113` still describes the Slack reminder as the sweep's trigger and is deliberately left — a point-in-time record.
- **Nineteen Minor review findings, triaged into two piles, and the smaller pile is recorded here rather than silently discarded.** Nine were fixed in one wave after the last task landed, all of them false or imprecise claims: a completeness docstring whose arithmetic was the pre-branch total (the module builds eight endpoints — six asserted there, the other two by named siblings), a comment saying a ledger-summed counter DECREASES on a correction when one that raises the total resets nothing, an un-tensed `T0044` citation, a test docstring asserting its producer's contents, another claiming a required field while pinning the parameter, a docstring template dropping `infra/runbooks/` from the link shape, a skill blanket describing the wrong cell of the register row, and two module docstrings asserting CI and alert-rule facts they cannot check. Nine were dropped, each with its reason: `\bT\d{4}\b` flags a compact time-of-day (`T0600 UTC`) — a class the design names and routes; `Phase[ -]\d` is case-sensitive and misses `phase 2` — a reach gap, not a precision one; the journal clause reads `reminders none read` for both "nothing to report" and "unreadable" — the pre-existing convention for every source, terse but never false; a NaN counter value reads as "unchanged" — not a shape `sum(increase())` emits; `except (*_UNREACHABLE, AttributeError)` omits `TypeError` — unreachable against real healthchecks.io JSON; the test slicer keys on the exact heading while the parser uses `startswith` — fails loudly; `or not resets` is unpinned — mutating it away still reaches unreadable via `IndexError`; "the paragraph is pasted into the journal" — true today and corroborated by the method's own name; and one probe-report sentence that overstates, living only in a workspace deleted at closeout.

## 2026-08-31 — iter-158: ten autonomous inductions against production, and what the fleet said back (spec 00105, [[T0158]])

- **The program shipped and then ran.** Spec `00105`'s telemetry tier became two runbook pages (`drills-telemetry.md`, `drills-order-path.md`), `docs/reference/drill-log.md` with a heading/order shape test, the critical rule `zcrypto-engine-dark-with-exposure` and its `engine.md` section, the `grafana-cloud-dark` procedure, index rows and the master plan's Stage-6b artifact line — and then **ten autonomous inductions were run against the live fleet**, one log entry each: J′, K, O, I, Q-logs, C-ops, C-secondary, C′, N and R are run, recorded and `pass`. **Counting INDUCTIONS, not drill letters** — C is one letter and two inductions, and **P is the tier's remaining letter and did not run at all**: its restore is a converge, so it stays attended and unproven, as the proven-tier table records; none `fail` or `blocked`; the fleet restored and verified **by value** after each. **R's entry was written last, deliberately** — `drills-telemetry.md` orders it written once, after the reconciler books R's hour, because until then the primary-whole read is *pending* and the four statuses carry no word for a pending verdict. It booked at 22:12:28Z: `residual_gap` flat, the secondary's `trade_deficit_rows` the only counter that moved, and the move attributed to hour 20 from the ledger's own `hour` field rather than from the tick that carried it.
- **Drill O closed a standing question rather than confirming a belief.** `ops-node.md` had carried the panel timer's missing staleness rule as an open gap; the dead-man alone caught a dead timer at exactly `timeout 7200 + grace 3600` from the last clean ping, 2 h 48 m 59 s of operator notice. **No staleness rule is owed**, and that paragraph is re-tensed from "exists to establish" to what was established — including the cost the gap buys, which was assumed before and is measured now.
- **Drill C had to be run in both halves or it would have shipped a false general rule.** On ops the logs plane replayed the outage in full (8 owed, 8 recovered, derived from the timers' own `OnCalendar`) while metrics recovered nothing. On the secondary **neither** plane recovered — its capture daemon direct-ships and Alloy is its only journal producer, which is the thing stopped. So "a stopped shipper loses metrics and recovers logs" holds **only where something writes to the journal during the outage**: recovery is a property of the host's workload, not of Alloy. The ops half alone would have licensed a claim false on most hosts.
- **Drill R's finding is larger than its page: the silence rules cannot see a stopped daemon.** `zcrypto-capture-all-streams-silent` scopes both capture hosts with `for: 0s` and a `gt 120` bar, and returned **0 rows** across 20 minutes of a stopped secondary — a stopped daemon does not publish a large value, it stops publishing, the series vanishes, and `noDataState: OK` reads the absence as healthy. The two detectors cover **opposite** failures, and the hc.io dead-man is the sole detector of this one.
- **A dead-man whose fire path is an unlabelled `or on() vector(0)` sends no resolved notice at all.** Drill N's target went `Alerting` then `Normal (MissingSeries)`, and Grafana deletes such an instance rather than resolving it: the fire path is the unlabelled fallback, the healthy path a labelled `sum by (host)`, so on recovery the firing instance ceases to exist. An operator watching Slack sees the page and never sees it clear. Recorded as a standing rule — **confirm a clear from the rule state, never from channel silence** — and surfaced rather than filed; changing a critical rule's expression is a Grafana push.
- **Drill N also pages four rules, not the three the plan enumerated.** `zcrypto-reconcile-source-lag` fires on **both** mirror sources at once because the NAS pull feeds both, carries the most alarming summary in the set ("the fleet has no witness and the next primary gap is permanent") at `severity: warning`, and is met cold by anyone working from the plan.
- **`zcrypto-hcio-watchdog` has both of its branches proven, by inductions designed for other things** — value `1` at J′ and I (a check down), value `999` at K (the hc.io scrape itself dark, since the ops Alloy *is* that scrape). Check-down twice, hc.io-dark once; they are not equally evidenced and the log says so.
- **The margins are exactly `60 s − scrape residue`.** Drill K's six rules cleared their bounds by 69/59/49/9/9/9 s, and residue + margin is 60 s for every one — the whole spread is one group evaluation interval, so three of the six sit **under one tick** of their bound. Those three are tight, not proven roomy.
- **Six method traps found by running, each fixed on the operating surface rather than in a report** — the rule-state endpoint keys on **title, not uid**; a firing rule's `activeAt` is **firing-onset**, so the `Pending` read must be taken while pending; the history endpoint takes **epoch seconds** (milliseconds return HTTP 200 with empty frames, reading as "never fired"); rule titles must match **whole** (`archive-pull stalled` hits both `NAS ·` and `Ops ·`, so a substring watch books another host's page as yours); a check's bound anchors on its **`last_ping`**, not a timer's `LastTriggerUSec`; and restoring an `OnCalendar` timer fires a run immediately regardless of `Persistent=`, so the returning ping is the evidence and `LastTriggerUSec` is not.
- **The runbook was cleaned of run records mid-branch.** Seven full run-record blocks had accumulated in `drills-telemetry.md` — timestamps, hold durations, verdicts — every measurement of which already sat in `drill-log.md`. Its own standing rule says a run lands its **findings** in the section, not its record. Measurements now live once in the log, proof status in the proven-tier table beside the incident-proved rows, and only what changes how a drill is run stays in the section.
- **Where a third party measures the same event, its clock is the record.** R's page time was first taken from a 60 s watcher's first *observation* and was 30 s later than healthchecks.io's own notice — a notification cannot precede its detection. Corrected to hc.io's 19 m 50 s and 2 m 36 s, with the local poll kept as corroboration.
- **One measurement is owed and dated rather than left vague.** R's reconcile-ledger read cannot be taken until the `:12`/`:42` tick after H+2 h — 22:12Z for an hour-20 induction — and the counters are cumulative, so the discriminator is the **step** across that tick. The pre-booking baseline is recorded by value in the entry so the later read is a comparison, not a fresh guess.
- **Drill N carries a delivery gap, recorded rather than omitted.** The Slack connector returned 404 mid-run and MCP binds at session start, so the running session could not read the channel; a second session on the same machine supplied the four timestamps. One of them, 40 s, is the **new maximum across six drill records — fourteen samples in all** and sits outside the 31–39 s band this log had quoted four times — recorded as the outlier it is rather than rounded in.
## 2026-08-31 — iter-159: the red button — one command that flattens the whole account, and the defect ~1900 green tests could not see (spec 00106, [[T0159]] partial)

- **`zcrypto engine flatten` exists, and its default sends nothing.** The command (`cli/engine/flatten.py`, registered in `cli/engine/command.py`) cancels every resting order account-wide, closes every margin position with a reduce-only MARKET IOC order, and sells every non-EUR spot balance at market in two passes, so a coin with no EUR pair is sold to BTC and the BTC is then sold to EUR. **The polarity is deliberate: the plain invocation is a dry run** — it reads the account, prints every leg with its side, quantity, pair and estimate at the taker rate, every dust line and every refusal, and exits 0 having sent nothing. Only `--execute` writes, behind a `FLATTEN` typed at the controlling terminal and never read from stdin, with no `--yes`. There is no `--dry-run` flag, because a flag that means "do nothing" is the one that gets forgotten — the invocation an operator reaches by muscle memory or a half-remembered runbook must be the one that changes nothing.
- **The exit code is the whole verdict and never reads a leg's answer.** 0 the final snapshot is flat; 1 refused with nothing sent (kill file absent, no terminal, the word did not match, credentials missing); 2 something is still open, or the account-wide cancel itself failed; 3 the venue could not be reached or read **before the first write**, `cancel_all_orders()` being that boundary. **A read that fails after the first write is 2 and never 3** — and never 0 — because the account may already have moved and the only honest answer is "go look". `exit_code` derives it from the final snapshot plus those two write-side failures alone; the journal carries what each leg answered.
- **One predicate serves both the send decision and the residual verdict, so the two structurally cannot disagree.** `classify_balance` returns `flat`/`dust`/`residual` for a non-EUR spot balance, and both the sweep (deciding whether to send) and `judge_final` (deciding whether the account is flat) read it — a balance skipped as dust can therefore never also be reported as a residual, nor a sold balance be silently written off. Dust is a **spot-only** class: the engine's machine deliberately produces sub-`ordermin` margin remainders, and a remainder left open is exposure, so a margin closer is sent regardless of the floors and the venue rules on it.
- **Every book read is taken before the first write, `BTC/EUR` included.** `build_plan` prices each pair once at depth 1, and prices `BTC/EUR` whenever any spot leg routes through a `/BTC` pair, because the second spot pass sells exactly the BTC those legs produce — so pass two never needs a read after a write. A leg with no price is sized on the quantity floor alone and **sold**, never skipped as dust: the safe direction. A book read is the one pre-write read failure that does not abort, since aborting there would exit 3 with the kill file latched and the engine already stopped, over one illiquid pair's empty side.
- **The venue-mutating structural guard was widened on purpose, and proved by construction rather than by its corpus.** `test_the_venue_mutating_names_have_exactly_one_module` now allowlists `cli/engine/flatten.py` beside `executor.py` — a second venue-mutating module by design, because the engine's machine and the red button must never share code paths when the machine is what broke — and gained `cancel_all_orders` as a watched name. Its mutation probe SURVIVED, correctly: no non-allowlisted `cli/` module names that string, so the corpus is degenerate with respect to the mutation and killing it would mean shipping a fake offending module. It was proven instead by construction on today's tree — a one-line offender written into `cli/` turns it red naming the path, on the new name and on `submit_order` alike; removing it turns it green.
- **The host wrapper is the operator's only interface.** `/usr/local/sbin/zcrypto-flatten`, rendered from `infra/ansible/roles/engine/templates/zcrypto-flatten.sh.j2` by the engine role, root-owned and not world-readable: with `--execute` it writes the kill file first (creating and chowning `exec/` on a host that has never tripped a kill), stops the engine unit, waits for it to be inactive, and only then runs a one-off container from the same image digest the engine runs. **`--entrypoint zcrypto`, placed before the image, is load-bearing** — the image's own ENTRYPOINT is capture's `sh -c` script, so without the override the words `engine flatten` are appended to that script and the container starts **capture** on a host whose engine was just stopped with positions open. The **still-active refusal** stands beside it: if the unit has not gone inactive the wrapper refuses to start the container at all, rather than putting a second live client on the one trade key.
- **The unattended daily pass can never press it.** The `ops_daily` tier classifier is asserted to refuse both `zcrypto-flatten` and `zcrypto engine flatten` under every wrapping the fleet actually spells — the wrapper, its absolute path, `docker exec` into the engine, an `ssh` retargeting the host, the in-container form with its state directory — each paired with a READ command wearing the same wrapper, because "is not autonomous" is also what a classifier broken into refusing everything answers.
- **The command could not run, and ~1900 green tests said it could.** Every `KrakenSpotHttpClient.request_*` needs a running event loop — outside one it raises `RuntimeError: no running event loop`, inside one it returns a `Future` — and `run_flatten` was called synchronously, so **every invocation, `--execute` included, exited 3 on the first read and no request ever left the process**. Beside it, `OrderBook.bids`/`.asks` are methods read as attributes, so `list()` of a bound method raised a bare `TypeError` out of a function that promises to raise nothing. Neither the spec nor the plan contains the words `async`, `await`, `asyncio` or `event loop`. Fixed with one `asyncio.run` at the CLI boundary, an awaiting recorder propagating async up the whole read path, fakes that model the real library shapes, and three contract pins (one gated on `ZCRYPTO_VENUE_CONTRACT=1`, an explicit opt-in so a skip is a decision and not a venue outage read as coverage). The same CLI invocation before and after: `no running event loop` → `API error: EAPI:Invalid key`. Same exit code, different world.
- **What found it was classifying the test doubles — a task the plan did not contain.** `tests/test_engine_stub_fidelity.py` had been red since the button's first commit: eight doubles in the new suite that no table classified, and nothing in the plan landed them. Ruled into the wrapper task; the classification forced each double to be compared against its real type, which is where the `Future` and the `method_descriptor` surfaced. Seven of the eight are LIBRARY stand-ins and now owe the offered direction — a stub carrying a field the real class lacks fails no test and comes back wrong only against the live account, which on this module is the whole button reading a shape the venue never sends.
- **The plan's mutation probes were re-run on a clean tree at the branch's end: 33 probes, 32 KILLED, 1 SURVIVED** — the one above, predicted and explained by the plan. Every probe applied, every control was seen to fail, none needed repointing; the plan line that had licensed *copying* five verdicts from an earlier task's run was rewritten to forbid it, since a verdict describes the tree it was measured on and eight commits had landed since.
- **What this iteration did NOT prove.** **No call has ever been made against the live account.** The single request this code has issued left the process under a dummy key and was answered `EAPI:Invalid key` — that a request signs, leaves and is answered, and nothing more. The five read shapes are unconfirmed against the real venue until the attended read-only dry run runs **through the wrapper** and is recorded in `docs/reference/adapter-verification/`; **reduce-only MARKET on margin has never been sent from this repository on any order type** and is unproven until drill B's log entry reads a pass. The runbook section names both limits where the operator meets them, [[T0159]] stays `partial` for exactly that reason, and [[T0160]] was registered here for the nautilus bump whose side-enum `Option` change would make this command refuse to flatten rather than fail loudly.

## 2026-09-01 — iter-160: `rest-hold` — an order that stays, and the two rulings that came from the running code (spec 00108, [[T0018]] partial)

- **A third plan mode, and its units are the whole safety story.** `cli/engine/probeplan.py`'s mode vocabulary is now the public `MODES` — `execute`, `rest-cancel`, `rest-hold` — and a `rest-hold` intent carries two plan-declared fields: `offset_pct`, how far passive of the touch the order is priced, and `hold_minutes`, how long it rests. **`offset_pct` is a PERCENT: `5.0` is five percent.** The dangerous misreading is the quiet one — `_REST_CANCEL_OFFSET = 0.05` is a *fraction*, so an author copying that constant's shape writes `offset_pct: 0.05`, gets an order five hundredths of a percent off the touch, and it fills, on the mode built never to. The opposite slip is loud and harmless: `500.0` prices absurdly and never places. Three things answer it together — the percentage reading is fixed at one site, `probe-plan --check` echoes both fields back in words on the intent line an operator reads before placing anything (`_intent_floor_check`), and there is deliberately **no numeric floor** on `offset_pct` beyond strictly-positive, because drill A2's whole subject is a tight offset the market reaches while the engine is down and no honest lower bound exists before that drill runs. The refusals land at the parse wall beside `leverage`'s own range check, not in `plan_refusals`: both fields required on `rest-hold`, both refused on the other two modes, `hold_minutes` an int in 1–`_MAX_HOLD_MINUTES` (60), `offset_pct` strictly positive, `action` `open` only.
- **The defect this spec existed to prevent was a one-token change.** Every mode branch in the executor was written `== "rest-cancel"` or `!= "rest-cancel"`, and nothing pinned the vocabulary's membership — so adding `"rest-hold"` to the enum alone would have parsed cleanly and then run the new mode with full `execute` semantics: joining the touch, repricing as it moves, and at the time box crossing the spread with up to three marketable IOCs. On the live trade path, green. Five sites are now explicit about all three modes, and **the one that would have cost money reads `active.falling_back = active.intent.mode == "execute"`** — turned around, so a fourth mode added and wired nowhere else inherits the arm that cannot cross. `timebox_at` is per intent (`hold_minutes` for this mode, `_TIME_BOX` for the others) rather than the single fifteen-minute constant, which was too short for E′'s human phone leg and A1's reboot-plus-verify list.
- **Spec D5 was FALSE, and had to be re-ruled from the call sites rather than from a docstring.** The original ruling rested on `_reprice`'s own docstring, which describes its two *callers* and not its *reachability*: `_on_cancel_ack`'s unrequested arm runs `_reprice` for **any** venue-originated `OrderCanceled` or `OrderExpired` while the phase is not `ioc`, and tests nothing about crossing. Driven on the tree — a resting order, no cancel requested, one `OrderCanceled` — the executor submitted a **second** post-only GTC priced off the current touch, and would do so up to five times. That path barely matters for a mode that rests milliseconds; it matters entirely for one that rests up to an hour while the operator is being *trained* to cancel by hand in the Kraken web UI (drill F2's operator action is exactly that). **The ruling: for `rest-hold`, an unrequested cancel is terminal** — the intent finishes `rest_hold_venue_canceled` (or `partial` if anything filled) and nothing is resubmitted. The guard sits in `_on_cancel_ack`, not in `_reprice`, because `_reprice`'s other caller — the synchronous post-only rejection — is a real production surface that replaces an order which never rested, and a tight-offset intent needs that recovery to get resting at all.
- **No alert rule ships, and the spec records why one cannot be built on this gauge.** The rule first proposed — an order resting longer than the maximum `hold_minutes` a plan may legally declare — fails for two structural reasons, not for want of effort. The ceiling and the legal maximum are the same number: `timebox_at` is capped at 60 minutes and the publish runs after the pump on the same tick, so the last non-zero sample is at most 3600 s — a threshold above the cap can never be crossed and one below it fires on every lawful hold. And the condition such a rule would be *for* — an order orphaned at the venue — is precisely the state where the gauge reads **zero**: all three orphan routes halt the plan and null the active order, after which the gauge publishes 0.0 for every mode while the order rests at Kraken. A rule that cannot fire is worse than none, because it reads as coverage on a board. What covers the orphan is already deployed: all three paths log at CRITICAL in words, and `zcrypto-engine-error-logs` hoists the message itself onto the page.
- **Admitting a metric family is what puts it on the fault-signal docket — a second-order effect nobody planned for.** `tests/test_infra_alert_rules.py`'s `_admitted_series()` parses the capture keep-regex line itself and subtracts `NOT_A_FAULT_SIGNAL`, so the moment the family joined the Alloy allowlist the branch went red on "admitted but no alert rule queries it" — two tasks before the gauge existed, and with a cause that would have looked like a later task's. Resolved as the exclusion it is, with D7's two reasons recorded at the guard rather than in a spec nobody opens from there.
- **The gauge states its own blind spots, and its publish cannot end a running plan.** `zcrypto_exec_resting_order_age_seconds{mode}` is published from `on_timer` out of the engine's own state — no venue read, no nonce cost — and its HELP text says in words that it is the engine's belief rather than venue truth (a cancel that could not reach the venue leaves this at zero while the order may still rest at Kraken) and that nothing publishes while the engine is down. Both are properties of where the number comes from, so they belong on the surface an operator reads. `_publish_resting_age` is wrapped **whole**: the metrics helper's own try/except guards only the call, while everything a publish site computes around it sits bare inside `on_timer`'s catch-all, whose `except` sets `_plan = None; _active = None` — a `NameError` there, on a tick while a real order rests, would drop the plan and leave nothing tracking the order. A test makes the sink raise and asserts the plan still running and the intent still resting.
- **The gauge reads 0.0 while a cancel is outstanding, ruled deliberately rather than defaulted into.** During phase `cancelling` the engine has already asked for the order back, and both exits from that phase give zero anyway; publishing the age there would invert the signal the whole family carries. The family joined the capture role's remote-write `keep` allowlist and the panel row on the engine board (panel 67) in the same branch — an anchored allowlist drops anything it does not name, so a family published before it is admitted is simply lost.
- **Four guards were found unproven by mutation probing, and the same shape produced three of them: a boolean composite whose fixture satisfied both halves.** The cross-mode refusal held under `or` → `and` because its fixture declared *both* fields — the escaping input is a **half**-declared hold, `mode: execute` with `offset_pct` and no `hold_minutes`, which is exactly the silently-ignored request the test's own docstring described. `hold_minutes`' `isinstance(..., int)` half and `_parse_positive_number`'s type half were each held in place by nothing, since every fixture was an out-of-range int or a bool. The fourth is in the executor: the resting-age condition's `phase == "resting"` term survived until a fixture existed where the phase is `cancelling` while the order is still held. Each was closed with a new fixture rather than a widened claim. **One probe is recorded as SURVIVED and left that way**: the same condition's `placed_at is not None` term is structurally unreachable — `_enter` is the only writer of the `resting` phase and stamps `placed_at` in the same statement — so no fixture can make defect and correct behaviour differ, and inventing one would prove nothing.
- **What this iteration did NOT prove — and the two converges it owes.** **No `rest-hold` order has ever been placed at Kraken.** What is proven is unit-level, plus one offline `--check` read; the mode ships disarmed and reaches the engine only on an attended converge. The branch owes **two** of them, in this order: the **capture** role first, because the keep-regex change lives in `infra/ansible/roles/capture/files/config.alloy` and the primary's rules apply in full — a family admitted before it is published costs nothing, one published before it is admitted is lost — and then the **engine**, inside a 4-hourly inter-cycle gap. `infra/runbooks/drills-order-path.md`'s five order-path sections (A1, A2, E, F2, G) therefore stay `blocked` on that second converge rather than on the instrument, and its standing note is re-tensed to say so rather than deleted; [[T0158]] already holds the order-path tier's execution, so nothing new was deferred.
- **The operator surfaces landed with the mode.** `infra/runbooks/engine-procedures.md` gains the `rest-hold` plan shape beside Drill A's, with the units fixed in the sentence next to it and the `--check` line it produces shown rather than described, plus the three terminal outcomes to read back in the ledger — `rest_hold_expired` (the hold run to its end), `rest_hold_venue_canceled` (the venue's or the operator's own cancel, which the engine deliberately does not undo — a completed intent, not a fault) and `revoked` (the kill file, a disarm or quote silence took it mid-rest). `develop` was merged into the branch before those edits rather than after, so the runbook audit that landed in the meantime kept its rewrites instead of being reverted by a stale base.
